			</item>
		<item>
		<title>KnowledgeForge: mining gold from the ITSM ticket graveyard</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/knowledgeforge-mining-gold-from-the-itsm-ticket-graveyard/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Anmol Dhankhar]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Wed, 19 Aug 2026 20:36:50 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Advanced (300)]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Amazon Bedrock]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Technical How-to]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">887843aa4f6b53cf9fd0ddae24f0fa2e4162dfb1</guid>

					<description>KnowledgeForge mines resolved ITSM incident tickets into new knowledge base articles and automatically curates the existing library by deduplicating, quality-scoring, and improving content, using Amazon Bedrock, Amazon S3 Vectors, and AWS Step Functions in a multi-tenant, closed-loop pipeline.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;&lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/sample-knowledgeforge" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;KnowledgeForge&lt;/a&gt; is about mining gold from the IT Service Management (ITSM) ticket graveyard: the resolved incident tickets whose knowledge never reaches a knowledge base article. Enterprise IT support teams resolve thousands of tickets every month, and each one holds something useful: a symptom, a root cause, and the fix an engineer applied. That knowledge stays locked in ticket history, where the next engineer to hit the same problem won’t find it.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The knowledge base itself has the opposite problem. It grows, but it grows messy: duplicate articles pile up, content goes stale, and quality varies depending on who wrote each article and when. A support engineer searching for an answer wades through near-identical drafts, some accurate and some three product versions out of date.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;We built KnowledgeForge to work both sides of that gap. It mines resolved incident tickets for new articles. At the same time, it curates the existing knowledge base by sorting articles by type, removing duplicates, scoring their quality, and rewriting weak content. A knowledge manager reviews and approves the result, so a person still owns what goes live.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This post covers the AWS building blocks behind KnowledgeForge: &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock&lt;/a&gt; for generation and content improvement, &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/s3/features/vectors/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon S3 Vectors&lt;/a&gt;, a capability of Amazon Simple Storage Service (Amazon S3), for duplicate detection, and &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/step-functions/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Step Functions&lt;/a&gt; for orchestration. For each one, we explain why we chose it and link to the documentation. If you’re building a large-scale document-processing pipeline on &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/ai/generative-ai/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;generative AI&lt;/a&gt;, you can reuse these patterns.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="prerequisites"&gt;Prerequisites&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To deploy and follow along with this solution, you need:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;An AWS account with access to Amazon Bedrock, and access enabled for Anthropic Claude Sonnet 4.5 and &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/amazon-models/titan/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Titan Text Embeddings V2&lt;/a&gt; models.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Permissions to create the resources this solution uses: &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/s3/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon S3&lt;/a&gt; buckets and Amazon S3 Vectors indexes, AWS Step Functions state machines, &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/lambda/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Lambda&lt;/a&gt; functions, &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/ecs/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Elastic Container Service (Amazon ECS)&lt;/a&gt; services on &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/fargate/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Fargate&lt;/a&gt;, &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/dynamodb/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon DynamoDB&lt;/a&gt; tables, &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/sqs/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Simple Queue Service (Amazon SQS)&lt;/a&gt; queues, Amazon Bedrock guardrails, and AWS Key Management Service (AWS KMS) keys.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;The AWS Cloud Development Kit (AWS CDK) installed, and working familiarity with Amazon Bedrock, AWS Step Functions, Amazon ECS, and vector embeddings.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;The code from the &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/sample-knowledgeforge" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;aws-samples/sample-knowledgeforge&lt;/a&gt; repository.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="a-closed-loop-knowledge-base-lifecycle"&gt;A closed-loop knowledge base lifecycle&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;KnowledgeForge is two subsystems that feed each other. Generation turns clustered incident tickets into new draft articles. When a group of related tickets describes the same problem, the system writes a knowledge base article and a root cause analysis document from that cluster. Curation then takes every article, both newly generated and existing, through four steps: classify it by type, check for duplicates, score its quality, and improve weak content. Finished articles go to ServiceNow for knowledge-manager review.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The loop closes because curation stores a vector for every article, and generation reads those vectors back before writing anything new. The following diagram shows how the two subsystems connect.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/13/ML-21271-1.png" alt="Closed-loop lifecycle diagram: ingestion feeds generation and curation, then curation embeds every article as grounding for the next generation run" width="800"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;Figure 1: The closed-loop knowledge base lifecycle. Ingestion feeds generation and curation, a knowledge manager reviews the output, and curation embeds every article so generation can reuse those vectors as grounding on the next run.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;At a high level, an article moves through five stages:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Ingestion &lt;/strong&gt;– Resolved tickets and existing articles land in Amazon S3, tracked by a data catalog.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Generation &lt;/strong&gt;–&amp;nbsp;Clustered tickets become new draft articles on Amazon ECS with AWS Fargate.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Curation &lt;/strong&gt;–&amp;nbsp;Each article is classified, deduplicated, quality-scored, and improved through an AWS Step Functions workflow on AWS Lambda.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Human review &lt;/strong&gt;–&amp;nbsp;Enriched articles go to ServiceNow for knowledge-manager approval, and the decision is written back to Amazon DynamoDB.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Closed loop &lt;/strong&gt;–&amp;nbsp;Curation embeds every article into the Amazon S3 Vectors index, and generation reuses those vectors on the next run.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The two subsystems run on different compute, for reasons the next sections explain as they follow an article through the system.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="generating-articles-from-incident-clusters"&gt;Generating articles from incident clusters&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Generation starts with a cluster of tickets that share a theme. An upstream process groups resolved incidents by the problem they describe and drops the result into an Amazon S3 bucket as a JSON file, scoped to one customer. Each theme carries keywords, an article scope, and a sample of ticket descriptions and work notes.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A new file in the bucket sends an event to an Amazon SQS queue. A container on Amazon ECS with AWS Fargate polls the queue, reads the file, and processes up to five themes at once for a customer.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Before writing, the system grounds itself in what exists. For each theme, it retrieves the five most similar existing articles from that customer’s Amazon S3 Vectors index and passes them to the model as reference context. This Retrieval Augmented Generation (RAG) keeps terminology consistent and reduces invented procedures. When no reference articles exist yet, the system generates from the ticket data alone and flags the procedures for review.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Generation runs on Anthropic Claude Sonnet 4.5 in Amazon Bedrock. For each theme, the model produces two documents with a fixed structure:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Knowledge base article&lt;/strong&gt;: Title and summary, symptoms, root cause, resolution steps, prevention, and related topics.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Root cause analysis document&lt;/strong&gt;: Executive summary, problem description, customer impact, five-why analysis, workaround and resolution, corrective and preventive actions, timeline of key events, and cause code.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;We use response streaming from Amazon Bedrock so the container assembles each document as tokens arrive rather than waiting for the full response.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="why-containers-instead-of-functions"&gt;Why containers instead of functions&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;We run generation on Amazon ECS with AWS Fargate because of the shape of the work. Generating two full documents for a theme can take several minutes, and a busy file holds many themes, so a single unit of work can run for a long time. The workload also arrives in bursts, quiet for stretches and then a large batch at once. A long-running container service that scales its task count on queue depth, and scales back when the queue drains, fits this pattern well.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/fargate/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Fargate&lt;/a&gt; matches this profile. It runs our containers serverlessly, scales on demand as themes arrive, and lets the team focus on the generation logic rather than on managing compute capacity. Each document lands in Amazon S3 as JSON, ready for curation.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="finding-duplicate-articles-with-amazon-s3-vectors"&gt;Finding duplicate articles with Amazon S3 Vectors&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The first curation challenge is detecting whether an article already exists. Duplicates come in several forms. Two articles describe the same fix in different words, a newer article supersedes an older one, or an engineer copies an article, changes two lines, and saves it as new.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;We chose Amazon S3 Vectors to solve this. It stores embedding vectors directly in Amazon S3, which avoids a standalone vector database. It keeps metadata alongside each vector for per-customer filtering and bills per query and per gigabyte rather than per running node. That makes it affordable to keep a vector for every article in the library. If you already store content in Amazon S3, you can add vector search without new infrastructure. For details, see the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon S3 User Guide&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Keyword matching misses these duplicate forms, so the pipeline matches on meaning. Every article gets a 1,024-dimension embedding from Amazon Titan Text Embeddings V2, stored in a per-customer Amazon S3 Vectors index. A vector store usually powers retrieval. Here the same index doubles as a duplicate detector. A new article is embedded, the index is queried for the nearest existing vectors, and anything inside a tight cosine-distance threshold counts as a duplicate. We start with a cosine distance of 0.05 (a similarity of 0.95 or higher) and a top-K of 5. We tuned the distance by sampling flagged pairs and tightening it until near-identical articles matched without catching merely related ones. A looser threshold produced false duplicates, and a tighter one missed reworded copies. The query is one call, filtered to the current customer and to active articles:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;response = s3vectors.query_vectors(
    vectorBucketName=VECTOR_BUCKET,
    indexName=VECTOR_INDEX,
    queryVector={"float32": embedding},
    topK=TOP_K + 1,  # +1 because the article can match itself
    returnMetadata=True,
    returnDistance=True,
    filter={"$and": [
        {"tenant_id": {"$eq": TENANT_ID}},  # per-customer isolation
        {"status": {"$in": ["UNIQUE", "PENDING"]}},  # ignore retired articles
    ]},
)
matches = [v for v in response.get("vectors", []) if v["key"] != article_id]&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;When the system finds a duplicate pair, it keeps the fresher article and retires the stale one instead of dropping the newcomer by default. The newest accurate version wins, which is the behavior a support engineer wants.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Reusing the index this way helps on retries too. Embeddings cost a model call to produce. A re-run reads the stored vector back instead of recomputing it, saving both time and Amazon Bedrock spend when a batch reruns.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="orchestrating-curation-at-scale-with-aws-step-functions"&gt;Orchestrating curation at scale with AWS Step Functions&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Curation runs over batches of articles that need orchestration to spread work across workers and recover from failures. AWS Step Functions provides that with a two-phase distributed map. It manages workflow state, applies the retries and error handling declared in the definition, and fans work across workers without custom coordination code. For how the distributed map works, see the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/step-functions/latest/dg/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Step Functions Developer Guide&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Two configuration choices are worth calling out. First, we set the item processor to &lt;code&gt;STANDARD&lt;/code&gt; rather than &lt;code&gt;EXPRESS&lt;/code&gt;. Each article makes long-running Amazon Bedrock calls that exceed the 5-minute &lt;code&gt;EXPRESS&lt;/code&gt; limit, and &lt;code&gt;STANDARD&lt;/code&gt; keeps a full execution history for debugging. Second, we point the &lt;code&gt;ItemReader&lt;/code&gt; at a manifest file in Amazon S3 rather than passing items inline, which keeps the workflow state small.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A daily schedule on &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/eventbridge/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon EventBridge&lt;/a&gt; starts the run. An AWS Lambda function finds customers with new or changed articles, groups the changes into batches, and places each batch on an Amazon SQS first-in-first-out (FIFO) queue. The FIFO queue orders batches per customer across runs, using the customer ID as the message group key, so different customers still run in parallel. Ordering across runs is only part of the story. Within a single execution, Phase 2 can process up to 40 batches concurrently, so FIFO alone doesn’t stop two duplicates in different batches from racing. What actually protects deduplication is that it runs sequentially within each batch inside the worker, while quality scoring and improvement run in parallel. Sequential dedup per batch, parallel quality and improvement, and FIFO for cross-run ordering together keep duplicate detection consistent.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A dispatcher function pulls one batch at a time and starts a Step Functions execution. The state machine runs in two phases, each a distributed map so the articles in a batch process concurrently:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Classify and embed &lt;/strong&gt;–&amp;nbsp;Each article is classified by type and given a vector embedding.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Deduplicate, score, and improve &lt;/strong&gt;–&amp;nbsp;The pipeline finds duplicates, scores quality, and improves content that falls below the threshold.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following diagram shows the trigger chain, the two phases, and the fault-tolerance mechanisms that protect a run.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/13/ML-21271-2.png" alt="Two-phase curation workflow: an Amazon SQS FIFO queue orders batches, article content passes as an Amazon S3 pointer, and a circuit breaker and dead-letter queue guard the run" width="800"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;Figure 2: Curation runs as a two-phase distributed map. Batches are ordered per customer through an Amazon SQS FIFO queue, article content is passed as an Amazon S3 pointer rather than inline state, and a circuit breaker and dead-letter queue keep a bad batch from stalling the run.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="passing-pointers-not-payloads"&gt;Passing pointers, not payloads&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A Step Functions execution carries state between states, capped at 256 KB. Knowledge base articles, with full HTML bodies, pass that limit quickly. Rather than thread article content through the state machine, we write the batch to Amazon S3 and pass only the location through the workflow. Each map worker reads what it needs directly from Amazon S3, so the state payload stays small no matter how large the articles are.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The distributed map reads its work items from a manifest in Amazon S3 rather than from inline state. A tolerated-failure percentage lets the run absorb a few bad articles without aborting the whole batch:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-json"&gt;"Phase1_ClassifyEmbed": {
    "Type": "Map",
    "ItemProcessor": {
        "ProcessorConfig": { "Mode": "DISTRIBUTED", "ExecutionType": "STANDARD" }
    },
    "ItemReader": {
        "Resource": "arn:aws:states:::s3:getObject",
        "ReaderConfig": { "InputType": "JSON" },
        "Parameters": { "Bucket.$": "$.bucket", "Key.$": "$.manifest_key" }
    },
    "MaxConcurrency": 40,
    "ToleratedFailurePercentage": 10
}&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="recovering-from-failures"&gt;Recovering from failures&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Two mechanisms keep a bad batch from taking down a run:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Dead-letter queue &lt;/strong&gt;–&amp;nbsp;A batch that fails repeatedly is set aside for investigation instead of blocking the queue behind it.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Circuit breaker (best effort) &lt;/strong&gt;– After three consecutive failures, the dispatcher stops starting new batches and resets the affected articles to their starting state. The failure counter lives in the memory of a warm Lambda function, so it isn’t shared across concurrent dispatcher containers and resets when a new job starts. Under interleaved multi-tenant load the breaker might not trip, so we treat it as a best-effort backstop rather than a guarantee. Nothing is lost either way: the next scheduled run picks up reset articles, and a tripped breaker signals that a service dependency needs attention first.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="making-amazon-bedrock-reliable-at-scale-and-improving-article-quality"&gt;Making Amazon Bedrock reliable at scale, and improving article quality&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The engineering effort goes into running tens of thousands of model calls concurrently against shared service quotas. The pipeline reaches Anthropic Claude Sonnet for generation and improvement, and Amazon Titan Text Embeddings V2 for deduplication vectors, all through Amazon Bedrock model inference. For model availability by AWS Region, see &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/models-regions.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Supported models by AWS Region&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="keeping-model-calls-inside-their-limits"&gt;Keeping model calls inside their limits&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Three controls keep generation predictable under load:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;A hard wall-clock timeout&lt;/strong&gt; on each execution, so a slow response cannot hold a worker open indefinitely.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Automatic retry with backoff &lt;/strong&gt;–&amp;nbsp;The Amazon Bedrock client runs in adaptive mode, retrying on its own when it meets throttling, with exponential backoff on longer content-improvement calls.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;A guardrail fallback &lt;/strong&gt;–&amp;nbsp;We run an Amazon Bedrock guardrail on model output to filter unintended content. The guardrail is a production control, and the following fallback is a resilience measure that keeps it in force under normal conditions rather than a signal that it is optional.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The guardrail API has its own rate limit. If it throttles, the pipeline processes the article without the guardrail and logs a warning instead of failing, which keeps things moving during a spike. In code, the fallback is a short piece of the model-call wrapper:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;try:
    return converse_with_timeout(kwargs, BEDROCK_CALL_TIMEOUT)
except ClientError as e:
    if "ThrottlingException" in str(e) and guardrail_config:
        log.warn("Guardrail throttled, retrying without guardrail")
        del kwargs["guardrailConfig"]
        return converse_with_timeout(kwargs, BEDROCK_CALL_TIMEOUT)
    raise&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A fourth control handles truncated output. Content-improvement responses have a token budget, and a long article can hit it, leaving the JSON response cut off. The system detects the “stopped at token limit” signal and retries with double the budget, up to the model’s ceiling. Short articles never trigger this path, so the common case pays nothing for the safety net.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="scoring-quality-before-and-after-improvement"&gt;Scoring quality before and after improvement&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Not every article needs improvement, and improvement does not always help. A quality score decides. Each article is scored across ten weighted dimensions, from the highest weight to the lowest:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Completeness&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Actionability&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Structure&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Coherence&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Readability&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Self-service value&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Freshness&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Automation readiness&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Grammar&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Security&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The weighted total produces one score, and a threshold decides whether the article meets the bar as-is or goes forward for improvement. The threshold is configurable, so a customer can hold generated content to a higher bar than imported content.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Articles below the threshold go through content improvement, also on Amazon Bedrock. This step has a practical challenge: a model asked to improve prose will often change image tags and hyperlinks too, and break them. Placeholders solve it. Before improvement, the system swaps every image and link for a numbered token, improves the prose around those tokens, and swaps the real media back afterward. The model never sees the raw markup, so headings and structure survive.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In code, this is a pair of functions that bracket the improvement call. The first replaces every media tag and link with a numbered token and records what it removed. The second puts the originals back after the model returns:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;# Before improvement: swap media/links for [IMG_N] / [LINK_N] tokens
html_with_placeholders, placeholder_map = extract_placeholders(article_html)

enriched = improve_with_bedrock(html_with_placeholders)  # model never sees raw markup

# After improvement: restore the exact original tags in place
restored_html, missing = restore_placeholders(enriched, placeholder_map)&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Because the model only ever sees &lt;code&gt;[IMG_1]&lt;/code&gt; or &lt;code&gt;[LINK_3]&lt;/code&gt;, it cannot corrupt a URL or an image reference. The restore step keeps the published article’s media exactly as it started.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;After improvement, the article is scored again. The new version is kept only when its score beats the original. Otherwise the original stands. That before-and-after check lets the pipeline improve content automatically without quietly making the library worse.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="serving-many-customers-safely"&gt;Serving many customers safely&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The system runs many customers on shared infrastructure, and a knowledge base is exactly the data customers expect kept separate. Isolation is designed in from the start: each customer gets its own copy of everything that touches its content:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;A configuration profile&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;An Amazon S3 Vectors index&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;A set of model prompts&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;An Amazon Bedrock guardrail&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;An &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/kms/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS KMS&lt;/a&gt; encryption key&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Because those resources are per-customer, each customer’s data stays within its own boundary. A duplicate check runs against that customer’s index alone, content improvement uses that customer’s prompts, and data is encrypted under that customer’s key. No shared pool exists where one customer’s articles could surface in another’s results.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Onboarding a customer doesn’t mean editing a central list. The system discovers customers from the data catalog at run time, so adding one only means provisioning its resources, with no change to the pipeline code.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="results-and-lessons-learned"&gt;Results and lessons learned&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The behavior here comes from our internal testing, not a guaranteed service level. Because each customer flows through its own Amazon SQS FIFO message group, adding a customer adds a parallel stream of work rather than slowing the others. Your own throughput will depend on article size, model latency, and your Amazon Bedrock quotas.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A representative run showed the pattern you can expect. Most articles came through fully processed on the first pass, a small share were held for improvement, and a smaller share were removed as duplicates. The run completed with no dead-letter-queue messages and no timeouts. A handful of articles hit the content-improvement token limit, were reset automatically, and were retried with a higher budget on the next run rather than failing. That last case matters, because it shows the resilience controls turning what would otherwise be hard failures into automatic retries.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Four lessons stand out. Reusing the vector index as a duplicate detector turned a hard problem into a nearest-neighbor query. Passing an Amazon S3 pointer through Step Functions instead of the article body removed a class of state-size failures. Scoring quality on both sides of improvement made it safe to run automatically. Treating a guardrail throttle as a warning kept the pipeline healthy through load spikes. The larger point is the closed loop itself: generated content feeds curation, and curation grounds the next round of generation, so the knowledge base improves as the system runs.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="clean-up-resources"&gt;Clean up resources&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The resources in this solution are billable, so remove them when you finish to avoid ongoing charges. Deleting the two AWS CDK stacks, one for knowledge base generation and one for knowledge base curation, removes most of them. That includes the AWS Lambda functions, the AWS Step Functions state machine, the Amazon ECS service on AWS Fargate, the Amazon SQS queues, and the Amazon DynamoDB tables.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A few resources need manual attention because they hold data or are created per customer:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Amazon S3 Vectors indexes and vector buckets &lt;/strong&gt;–&amp;nbsp;Delete each per-customer index, then the vector bucket.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Amazon S3 buckets &lt;/strong&gt;–&amp;nbsp;Empty and delete the source, output, and pipeline buckets.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Amazon Bedrock guardrails &lt;/strong&gt;–&amp;nbsp;Delete the per-customer guardrails if the stack leaves them in place.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;AWS KMS keys &lt;/strong&gt;–&amp;nbsp;Schedule deletion of the per-customer keys.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Amazon DynamoDB tables &lt;/strong&gt;–&amp;nbsp;Confirm the metrics and article-status tables are removed if you disabled point-in-time recovery retention.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;See the repository README for the exact deletion commands.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The ITSM ticket graveyard is a knowledge problem that looks like a data problem. The resolutions already exist, trapped where nobody can reuse them, next to a knowledge base that decays faster than any team can hand-curate it. KnowledgeForge closes that gap with generative AI on AWS. It mines tickets into articles with Amazon Bedrock, catches duplicates with Amazon S3 Vectors, and orchestrates the flow with AWS Step Functions. A knowledge manager stays in control of what goes live.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To get started building a similar pipeline:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Create a vector index using the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon S3 Vectors documentation&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Embed a sample of your own articles and load the vectors.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Run the nearest-neighbor query shown earlier to flag duplicates.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Add the AWS Step Functions distributed map to batch-process articles.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Layer on the score-improve-rescore quality gate.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The complete code is available in the &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/sample-knowledgeforge" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;aws-samples/sample-knowledgeforge&lt;/a&gt; repository on GitHub. The code samples throughout show the load-bearing parts of each pattern, ready to adapt to your own knowledge base.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To learn more, see the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock User Guide&lt;/a&gt;, the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/step-functions/latest/dg/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Step Functions Developer Guide&lt;/a&gt;, and the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon S3 User Guide&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt;

		<pubDate>Tue, 18 Aug 2026 18:56:14 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Amazon Bedrock]]></category>

		<guid isPermaLink="false">12f1da7fc2b3e4a6e9ccd34bd88bf557109f7e0f</guid>

					<description>Amazon Bedrock AgentCore payments is now generally available, enabling AI agents to autonomously transact at scale with built-in spending guardrails, protocol-agnostic payment orchestration, and production-ready observability.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;Agents have evolved from simple chat applications to autonomous, long-running systems that dynamically discover and compose dozens of tools per task without human oversight. On the other side, service and content providers are moving from human-centric subscription-based, one-size-fits-all pricing to pay-per-use, per-execution models where costs are often a few cents. Today, agents are doing a great job at reasoning, choosing tools, and completing tasks, but they hit a roadblock when payment is involved. To address this, we launched AgentCore payments in collaboration with Coinbase and Stripe in preview in May, enabling agent developers to equip their agents to autonomously pay for paid APIs, MCPs, and content with a few lines of code. Today, we are happy to announce that this service is generally available, enabling enterprises to power agentic payments with security, guardrails, and observability for production workloads.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;figure&gt;
 &lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/11/ML-21677-1.png" alt="Architecture diagram showing the AgentCore payments workflow" width="800"&gt;
&lt;/figure&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="key-features"&gt;Key features&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="wallet-support"&gt;Wallet support&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Agents need a source of funds to make agentic transactions. AgentCore payments addresses this by integrating with Coinbase and Stripe Privy wallets, enabling developers to set up payment capabilities by providing Coinbase or Privy API keys and secrets. These are stablecoin wallets purpose-built for cost-effective microtransaction payments, often in cents. End users can fund their agent’s wallet through traditional payment methods like credit cards or through USDC stablecoin and must grant delegation to the agent to spend on their behalf. To verify security, AgentCore stores developer credentials in the AgentCore Identity Secrets Manager. The agent does not see the raw credentials. AgentCore payments uses short-lived tokens derived from these credentials to instruct the wallet provider to perform wallet operations (such as transaction signing) as directed by the developer’s agent, adding an extra layer of security for the interactions.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For GA, we built a “Quick Create” option for Coinbase directly within AgentCore Payments (available through the console or CLI) that enables developers to provision credentials without leaving AgentCore, saving developers time and effort. For Stripe Privy, developers need to obtain the credentials from the &lt;a href="https://docs.privy.io/authentication/overview#api-authentication" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Privy dashboard&lt;/a&gt; and provide those to AgentCore payments.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 640px;" class="wp-video"&gt;
 &lt;video class="wp-video-shortcode" id="video-137241-1" width="640" height="360" preload="metadata" controls="controls"&gt;
  &lt;source type="video/mp4" src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/artifacts/DBSBlogs/ML-21677/ACPaymentsQuickCreate3.mp4?_=1"&gt;
 &lt;/video&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="payment-orchestration"&gt;Payment orchestration&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;AgentCore payments is built to be protocol-agnostic, abstracting away the complexity so developers can integrate once and work with merchants regardless of which protocol they use. At preview, we launched with support for the x402 protocol. At GA, we have expanded protocol support to include the Machine Payment Protocol (MPP), another standard for machine payments co-authored by Stripe and Tempo, enabling developers to pay for any MPP-compatible service without an additional line of code. Additionally, we have introduced support for the “upto” scheme within x402, which enables an agent to set a spending ceiling rather than committing to a fixed price. This unlocks true pay-per-inference and dynamic pricing: a merchant serving LLM tokens, compute, or any usage-metered API can now charge for exactly what was consumed at the end of a call, rather than requiring a fixed price for requests (as with the “exact” scheme supported today).&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="discoverability-of-pay-per-use-endpoints"&gt;Discoverability of pay per use endpoints&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;AgentCore exposes pay-per-use x402 endpoints offered by Coinbase as an MCP server via AgentCore Gateway, enabling agent developers to discover and select relevant paid endpoints based on their use case. We have now enhanced this discovery experience by updating the MCP server to a curated list of high-quality endpoints based on social proof, metadata richness, description quality, and availability.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="payment-limits"&gt;Payment limits&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Agents are inherently non-deterministic, so they can misinterpret a response as authorization to spend or repeat a payment because of an unexpected retry. In AgentCore payments, transactions run within a payment session, a scoped payment context for a single agent interaction. A payment session has two configurable caps: a maximum spend amount in a specified currency, and an expiry time. Before signing a payment, AgentCore payments checks the request against the session budget and rejects requests that would push the session past its cap. The check is deterministic and runs at the infrastructure layer.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="observability"&gt;Observability&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;As agents execute autonomous transactions, developers need comprehensive observability—including payment audit trails, detailed logs, and key metrics such as success rates and average transaction values—to maintain visibility and audit their agents’ spending behavior. AgentCore payments integrates with &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/observability-payments-metrics.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AgentCore Observability&lt;/a&gt; to give developers visibility into the payment lifecycle and automatically emits vended logs to your Amazon CloudWatch log group, and vended spans to AgentCore Observability. AgentCore Observability includes prebuilt dashboards that show end-to-end transaction health (for example, transaction success rate, average transaction value) across agents, payment sessions, and time periods.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="use-cases"&gt;Use cases&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="pay-for-paid-web-content"&gt;Pay for paid web content&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Publishers hosting content on the web today are blocking agent access and monetizing agent traffic. Enterprises deploying AI agents that access the web in real time, whether for information retrieval or web-based automation, AI browser tool providers, and agentic web search providers would benefit from integrating with the AgentCore payments service, which enables their agentic solutions to pay for paywalled web content without breaking the agent workflow. To enable this, we continue to collaborate with Content Delivery Networks (CDN) including Amazon CloudFront and Cloudflare. One of our customers, &lt;a href="https://anchorbrowser.io/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Anchor Browser&lt;/a&gt;, integrated with AgentCore payments to unlock paywalled web content for their customers.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;blockquote&gt;
 &lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;“Anchor Browser is one of the leading cloud-based browser automation platforms for enterprises with millions of traffic per day, purpose-built for AI agents. We are excited to integrate AgentCore payments to enable our customers to unlock paid web content within their web-based agentic workflows. The integration was smooth, and it offers a secure way to handle agentic transactions with built-in payment limits.”&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
 &lt;p&gt;— Idan Raman, CEO, Co-Founder of Anchor Browser&lt;/p&gt; 
 &lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;“Publishers spent years building paywalls. Now AI agents need to get through them. Amazon CloudFront and AWS WAF give content owners a single control plane for AI traffic — which agents can access what content, at what price, and for what use — with payment verified at the edge over the open x402 protocol. Agents built on AgentCore can pay to access this content without breaking their workflow. Content owners enable agent traffic on their terms — that changes the conversation from defense to commerce.” &lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
 &lt;p&gt;— Nishit Sawhney, General Manager, AWS Edge Services&lt;/p&gt; 
 &lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;“Cloudflare sits in front of a significant share of the world’s content, APIs, and MCP servers. Our Monetization Gateway lets owners of those assets charge agents directly, per request and at a price they set using the open x402 protocol. With AgentCore payments, enterprises building agents on AgentCore can access and pay for these assets on day one.”&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
 &lt;p&gt;— Stephanie Cohen, Chief Strategy Officer, Cloudflare&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/blockquote&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="pay-per-inference"&gt;Pay per inference&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Pay-per-inference is an emerging use case for agentic transactions, enabling agent developers to dynamically switch between models to optimize for cost without compromising on quality and the overhead of managing API keys and subscriptions with individual model providers. One of our customers, SpreadX, used AgentCore Payments in their new AI product, Incarna, to pay for inference through BlockRun — a routing and payment layer where agents pay per call over the x402 protocol.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;blockquote&gt;
 &lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;“We’re thrilled to collaborate with AgentCore payments, which offers a managed service that makes it easy for agents to securely access and pay for BlockRun’s pay-per-use endpoints, unlocking a whole new pay-per-inference use case.”&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/blockquote&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;— Vicky Fu, Founder and CEO, BlockRun&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="consumer-and-deep-research-use-case"&gt;Consumer and deep research use case&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Travala, a travel provider, has integrated AgentCore payments into their Travala Travel MCP servers, enabling end customers using agentic platforms like Claude to safely and securely book hotels conversationally in a single chat. Similarly, Elsa AI and Heurist AI use AgentCore payments to deliver financial research and advisory to their end customers by accessing pay-per-use APIs over the x402 protocol.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;blockquote&gt;
 &lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;“Integrating AgentCore payments into the Travala Travel MCP streamlines the agentic booking process for our 2.2 million properties globally. This integration offers a secure way to handle agentic transactions with built-in payment limits, improving the overall agentic booking experience for our customers.”&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/blockquote&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;— Juan Otero, CEO of Travala&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="get-started-with-agentcore-payments"&gt;Get started with AgentCore payments&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;AgentCore payments is available in the following &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/agentcore-regions.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;regions&lt;/a&gt;. Developer experience and choice is the foundational design principle of AgentCore payments. With ready-to-use skills, an intuitive console, and integrations with popular open-source frameworks, we make it straightforward for developers to build with AgentCore payments so they can stay focused on what matters most: &lt;em&gt;the quality and performance of their buyer agents securely at scale.&lt;/em&gt; You can set up and use AgentCore payments through multiple options:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="coding-assistant-skill"&gt;Coding assistant skill&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Whether you are building a transacting agent for the first time or adding autonomous payment capabilities to an existing agent, point your coding assistant (Claude Code, Kiro, Codex) to the skill. Your coding assistant will have the full context it needs to scaffold payment-enabled agents — from credential setup to transaction execution — so your agent can autonomously pay for paid service or content out of the box. For more details, refer to the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/payments-getting-started.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Quick start guide&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://github.com/aws/agent-toolkit-for-aws/tree/main/plugins/aws-agents" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Agent toolkit plugin&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://github.com/awslabs/agentcore-samples/tree/main/01-features/08-agents-that-transact/01-payments-skills-and-cli" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Payment Skills &amp;amp; CLI Samples&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="agentcore-cli"&gt;AgentCore CLI&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Install the AgentCore CLI to scaffold, configure, and deploy fully transacting agents in a few commands. This wires up payment capabilities — including credential provisioning, transaction routing — giving you an end-to-end payment-enabled agent ready to transact in minutes, not days. For more details, refer to the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/payments-getting-started.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Quick start guide&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="aws-management-console"&gt;AWS Management Console&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;You can get started with the &lt;a href="https://console.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore console&lt;/a&gt;. In the navigation pane, under &lt;strong&gt;Build&lt;/strong&gt;, choose &lt;strong&gt;Payments&lt;/strong&gt;. The &lt;strong&gt;Quick Create&lt;/strong&gt; option provides a way to create your Coinbase credentials on the console and set up your AgentCore payments resources.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="open-source-framework-integrations"&gt;Open source framework integrations&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;When building agents that transact, developers need to handle the HTTP &lt;em&gt;402 Payment Required&lt;/em&gt; response intercepted within their agent framework’s execution loop. AgentCore payments works with popular open source frameworks and any model. AgentCore payments provides integrations that add payment capabilities with minimal code changes. Explore the framework-specific implementations in the following section:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Strands Agents plugin&lt;/strong&gt; 
  &lt;ul&gt; 
   &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://strandsagents.com/docs/integrations/plugins/agentcore-payments/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Documentation&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/li&gt; 
   &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://github.com/awslabs/agentcore-samples/blob/main/01-features/08-agents-that-transact/00-getting-started/01-agents-payments-and-limits/strands_payment_agent.py" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Example&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/li&gt; 
  &lt;/ul&gt; &lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre style="padding-left: 80px"&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;plugin = AgentCorePaymentsPlugin(config=AgentCorePaymentsPluginConfig(
    payment_manager_arn=os.environ["PAYMENT_MANAGER_ARN"],
    user_id="test-user-123",
    payment_instrument_id=os.environ["PAYMENT_INSTRUMENT_ID"],
    payment_session_id=os.environ["PAYMENT_SESSION_ID"],
))
agent = Agent(model=BedrockModel(model_id=MODEL_ID, streaming=True), tools=[], plugins=[payment_plugin], system_prompt=SYSTEM_PROMPT)&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;LangGraph middleware&lt;/strong&gt; 
  &lt;ul&gt; 
   &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://docs.langchain.com/oss/python/integrations/middleware/aws#agentcore-payments" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Documentation&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/li&gt; 
   &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://github.com/awslabs/agentcore-samples/blob/main/01-features/08-agents-that-transact/00-getting-started/01-agents-payments-and-limits/langgraph_payment_agent.py" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Example on AgentCore&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/li&gt; 
   &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://github.com/langchain-samples/langchain-aws-samples/tree/main/examples/agentcore-payments" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Example with LangSmith&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/li&gt; 
   &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://www.langchain.com/blog/langchain-agentcore-payments"&gt;Blog post&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/li&gt; 
  &lt;/ul&gt; &lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre style="padding-left: 80px"&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;payments = AgentCorePaymentsMiddleware(config)
agent = create_agent(model, tools=[], system_prompt=SYSTEM_PROMPT, middleware=[payments])&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;OpenClaw plugin&lt;/strong&gt; 
  &lt;ul&gt; 
   &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://clawhub.ai/packages/@aws/aws-agents-pay" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Explore ClawHub&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/li&gt; 
   &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://github.com/awslabs/agentcore-samples/tree/main/01-features/08-agents-that-transact/01-payments-skills-and-cli/converse-with-openclaw-agent/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Example&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/li&gt; 
   &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/build-openclaw-agents-that-transact-with-amazon-bedrock-agentcore-payments/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Blog post&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/li&gt; 
  &lt;/ul&gt; &lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;blockquote&gt;
 &lt;p&gt;The &lt;em&gt;agents-pay&lt;/em&gt; skill enables an agent to pay for x402-protected content at runtime — intercepting a 402 Payment Required paywall mid-task via AgentCore payments, allowing autonomous task completion without human intervention.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/blockquote&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Today, AgentCore payments is generally available, marking a step in making the agentic economy a reality, where AI agents don’t just reason and act, but transact autonomously, securely, and at scale. By abstracting the complexity of payment protocols, wallet management, and transaction orchestration, we enable developers to focus on building intelligent agents while AgentCore handles the infrastructure. Our customers are already proving the breadth of what’s possible when payments become a native, first-class capability of the agent stack. With protocol-agnostic support, built-in spending guardrails, and production-ready observability, AgentCore payments gives developers the confidence to deploy transacting agents in production in minutes, not days. Get started today and equip your agents to transact.&lt;/p&gt;

		<item>
		<title>Customize Amazon Quick embedded chat into your application</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/customize-amazon-quick-embedded-chat-into-your-application/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Chetan Makvana]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Tue, 18 Aug 2026 17:13:13 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Advanced (300)]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Amazon Quick Suite]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Technical How-to]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">950b40d5dbbd76bbebf419549700238549cd23fa</guid>

					<description>Amazon Quick embedded chat brings a conversational AI interface into your web application. This post walks through customizing the embedded chat with container and SDK styling, branding removal, and a custom agent persona so it matches your brand's look, feel, and voice.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;&lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/quicksuite/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Quick&lt;/a&gt; embedded chat provides a conversational AI interface that you can integrate directly into your web application. Your users can ask questions, explore data, and get insights without leaving your application. However, a generic chat interface creates a disjointed experience. The chat interface must look and feel like a natural part of your application, not an external addition. With Quick customization capabilities, you can extend your organization’s visual theming and brand voice to match your application’s look and feel, providing a consistent and branded experience.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In this post, we walk through the configuration options for customizing Quick embedded chat to deliver a consistent, branded experience within your application.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="overview"&gt;Overview&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Customization requirements typically fall into two key areas. The first is visual theming to match the company’s brand. Your organization has established brand guidelines, and the embedded chat must reflect these guidelines. The second is tone to match the company’s voice because visual consistency alone isn’t enough. The way the chat communicates must also reflect your organization’s personality.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following example uses a financial analysis assistant embedded in a finance performance dashboard to demonstrate how to configure visual theming and tone customization.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/13/ML-20497-1.png" alt="Finance performance dashboard with the Amazon Quick embedded chat panel open on the right" width="800"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;Figure 1: Financial analysts dashboard with embedded Quick chat&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="configure-visual-theming"&gt;Configure visual theming&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;When you first embed the Quick chat into your application, the chat interface uses its default styling. This creates a visual mismatch. The chat looks like an external tool bolted onto your application rather than a native component. Notice the mismatched color palette, generic branding, and lack of visual integration with the surrounding finance dashboard.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Apply visual theming so the embedded chat feels like a natural part of the finance dashboard. Embedded chat visual theming operates at two levels. The first is container and layout styling, which is the CSS you control around the chat iframe. The second is SDK frame options, which is the configuration passed to the embedding SDK that controls the iframe behavior and branding elements.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Because the chat renders inside an iframe, you can’t style its internal elements directly. Instead, you style the container that wraps the iframe and use SDK options to remove default branding elements that conflict with your design.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="frame-options-configuration"&gt;Frame options configuration&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The &lt;code&gt;frameOptions&lt;/code&gt; parameter controls how the iframe behaves within your container:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-javascript"&gt;import { createEmbeddingContext } from 'amazon-quicksight-embedding-sdk';
const embeddingContext = await createEmbeddingContext();

const frameOptions = {
    url: chatEmbedUrl,
    container: document.getElementById('chat-container'),
    height: '100%',
    width: '100%',
    // Apply a custom CSS class directly to the iframe element.
    // Use this for border-radius, positioning, or z-index adjustments
    // to match your application's component design.
    className: 'anycompany-chat-iframe',
    // Display a loading placeholder while the chat content loads,
    // preventing a blank white rectangle during initialization.
    withIframePlaceholder: true,
    // Enable clipboard access so users can copy responses
    // or paste content into the chat input.
    framePermissions: {
        clipboardRead: true,
        clipboardWrite: true,
    },
};&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="container-and-layout-css"&gt;Container and layout CSS&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Style the container that wraps the chat to match your application’s design system. In our finance dashboard example, the chat appears as a side panel that slides in from the right.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-css"&gt;/* Position the chat as a fixed side panel */
.chat-side-panel {
    position: fixed;
    top: 0;
    right: 0;
    bottom: 0;
    width: 400px;
    z-index: 997;
    box-shadow: -4px 0 20px rgba(0, 0, 0, 0.15);
    animation: slideInRight 0.3s ease-out;
}

/* Brand-colored header matching the dashboard's gradient */
.chat-panel-header {
    background: linear-gradient(135deg, #667eea, #764ba2);
    color: white;
    padding: 12px 16px;
}

/* Target the iframe via the custom className from frameOptions */
.anycompany-chat-iframe {
    border-radius: 0 0 12px 12px;
}

/* Responsive: full-width on mobile */
@media (max-width: 900px) {
    .chat-side-panel {
        width: 100%;
    }
}&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="remove-default-branding"&gt;Remove default branding&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;By default, the embedded chat displays an Amazon Quick brand attribution and a usage policy link in the footer. For a brand theme experience where the chat appears as a native part of your application, remove these elements:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-javascript"&gt;const contentOptions = {
    footerOptions: {
        showBrandAttribution: false,
        showUsagePolicy: false,
    },
};&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;After applying the container styling, custom CSS class, and branding removal, the embedded chat now looks like a natural extension of the finance dashboard. The panel uses the same color gradient, the footer is clean, and the overall appearance is consistent with the application’s design system.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/13/ML-20497-2.png" alt="Finance dashboard with the embedded chat panel restyled to match the dashboard’s color gradient and branding" width="800"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;Figure 2: Financial analysts dashboard with visually matching embedded Quick chat&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following table provides a reference for the visual theming options and what each one controls.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Option&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;What it controls&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;className&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;CSS class on the iframe (border-radius, shadows, z-index)&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;width&lt;/code&gt; / &lt;code&gt;height&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Iframe dimensions (use “100%” for responsive layouts)&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;withIframePlaceholder&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Loading spinner while content loads&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;footerOptions.showBrandAttribution&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Show or hide the “Powered by” text&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;footerOptions.showUsagePolicy&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Show or hide the usage policy link&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Container CSS&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Panel positioning, borders, shadows, header styling&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="configure-chat-persona-and-tone"&gt;Configure chat persona and tone&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In addition to visual theming, the chat’s communication style must reflect your organization’s personality. Without persona configuration, the embedded chat responds with generic, broad answers that don’t match your company’s communication style or domain expertise.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/13/ML-20497-3.png" alt="Embedded Quick chat returning a generic response to a revenue question without organizational context" width="800"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;Figure 3: Financial analysts dashboard with embedded Quick chat showing a chat response&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Without a custom chat agent, responses lack organizational context. For example, in the finance dashboard, the question “What was our revenue last quarter?” produces a response like the following:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;blockquote&gt;
 &lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;“Revenue is a measure of income generated from business operations. To find your company’s revenue for last quarter, you would typically look at your financial statements or accounting system. Revenue can be broken down by product line, region, or time period.”&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/blockquote&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This response lacks specific context about your organization and uses a tone that doesn’t match how your finance team communicates. With the Quick &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/quick/latest/userguide/custom-agents.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;custom chat agent&lt;/a&gt;, you can define a persona that controls the tone, language style, and response behavior. You configure this through SDK content options and agent persona instructions. SDK content options control what UI elements appear and direct users to your custom agent. Agent persona instructions define the personality, response style, and knowledge boundaries (configured in the Quick console when you create the chat agent).&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="sdk-content-options"&gt;SDK content options&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-javascript"&gt;const contentOptions = {
    // Lock to your custom finance agent --- removes the generic agent selector
    // so users only interact with your purpose-built assistant
    agentOptions: {
        fixedAgentId: 'your-finance-agent-id',
    },
    promptOptions: {
        // Auto-send a contextual greeting on first load.
        // The assistant proactively shares relevant insights
        // rather than waiting passively for a question.
        initialPrompt: 'What are the key financial highlights this quarter?',
        // Hide the user message bubble for the initial prompt.
        // This makes it appear as if the assistant initiated the conversation,
        // creating a more natural "advisor" experience.
        showInitialPromptMessage: false,
        // Enable file attachments --- users can upload spreadsheets
        // or reports for the assistant to analyze
        allowFileAttachments: true,
        // Show web search for supplementary market research
        showWebSearch: true,
        // Show chat history so users can resume prior conversations
        showChatHistory: true,
        // Display knowledge boundary indicator --- builds trust by showing
        // users what data sources the assistant can access
        showAgentKnowledgeBoundary: true,
    },
};&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="agent-persona-instructions-quick-console"&gt;Agent persona instructions (Quick console)&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;When configuring your custom chat agent in the Amazon Quick console on the &lt;strong&gt;Chat Agents&lt;/strong&gt; page, you define persona instructions that shape how the agent responds. The following example shows a persona instruction block you can use in your agent’s configuration:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;pre class="text"&gt;&lt;code&gt;You are the AnyCompany Finance Assistant, an AI embedded in AnyCompany's
Finance Performance Dashboard.

## Your Role
You help executive leadership, finance teams, and business leads explore
financial data, understand performance trends, and take action on insights
--- all without leaving the dashboard.

## Response Style
- Lead with the direct answer and the key number
- Provide context and comparison to prior period
- Cite the source document when referencing qualitative information
- Use bullet points for multi-part answers
- Keep responses concise --- executives want the insight, not the explanation

## Boundaries
- Only discuss AnyCompany financial data and business context
- Do not speculate about future performance beyond stated targets
- For questions outside your knowledge, direct users to finance@anycompany.com&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/13/ML-20497-4.png" alt="Embedded Quick chat returning a direct, data-driven response that matches the finance team’s communication style" width="800"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;Figure 4: Embedded Quick chat showing a response that follows the configured communication style&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;With the custom finance agent configured, the same question “What was our revenue last quarter?” now produces a response that matches your organization’s communication style: direct, data-driven, and contextual.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;blockquote&gt;
 &lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;“Q2 2025 revenue was $8.89M, up 12.5% vs Q2 2024 ($7.05M). Growth was driven by consistent month-over-month momentum across all regions:&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
 &lt;ul&gt; 
  &lt;li&gt;&lt;em&gt;April: $2.89M&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/li&gt; 
  &lt;li&gt;&lt;em&gt;May: $2.95M&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/li&gt; 
  &lt;li&gt;&lt;em&gt;June: $3.05M&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;/ul&gt; 
 &lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;North America led with 15% growth, followed by EMEA at 9%.”&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/blockquote&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/13/ML-20497-5.png" alt="Embedded Quick chat response with a lead number, month-over-month breakdown, and regional growth" width="800"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;Figure 5: Quick chat response with the configured persona&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following table lists the persona customization options that you can configure while using the SDK.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Option&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Effect on persona/tone&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;fixedAgentId&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Locks to your custom agent with a specific personality&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;initialPrompt&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Sets the conversational opening: proactive or reactive&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;showInitialPromptMessage: false&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;The assistant appears to initiate, creating an “advisor” feel&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;showAgentKnowledgeBoundary&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Transparency about what the assistant knows&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Agent persona instructions&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Defines tone, vocabulary, response structure, and boundaries&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="programmatic-interaction"&gt;Programmatic interaction&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Beyond visual theming and persona, the SDK provides the &lt;code&gt;sendPrompt()&lt;/code&gt; method. With this method, your application can trigger contextual questions from UI interactions. For example, when a user selects a chart metric, the application sends a related question to the assistant. This connects your dashboard visuals with the conversational interface.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-javascript"&gt;// Store the experience reference after embedding
const chatExperience = await embeddingContext.embedQuickChat(
    frameOptions,
    contentOptions
);

// When a user selects a revenue metric card in your dashboard:
chatExperience.sendPrompt(
    'Explain the revenue trend this quarter and what is driving it.'
);&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In the finance dashboard example, quick-action buttons in the chat panel header implement this pattern:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-javascript"&gt;const askAboutMetric = (metricName) =&amp;gt; {
    chatExperience.sendPrompt(
        `Explain the trend for ${metricName} and what's driving it.`
    );
};

// Quick-action buttons
const metrics = [
    { metric: 'revenue', label: 'Revenue' },
    { metric: 'profit margin', label: 'Margins' },
    { metric: 'expenses', label: 'Expenses' }
];

const container = document.getElementById('quick-actions');
metrics.forEach(({ metric, label }) =&amp;gt; {
    const button = document.createElement('button');
    button.type = 'button';
    button.textContent = label;
    button.setAttribute('aria-label', `Ask about ${metric}`);
    button.addEventListener('click', () =&amp;gt; askAboutMetric(metric));
    container.appendChild(button);
});&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/13/ML-20497-6.png" alt="Embedded Quick chat panel with a quick-action Revenue button in the header" width="800"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;Figure 6: Quick chat with a Revenue action button&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In this post, we showed how to customize the Quick embedded chat interface to deliver a consistent, branded experience within your application. By configuring CSS, you align the chat panel visually with your design system so it appears as a native component rather than an external widget. By defining agent persona instructions and SDK prompt options, you can shape the chat’s communication style to match your brand voice. The &lt;code&gt;sendPrompt()&lt;/code&gt; method bridges your application’s UI with the conversational interface by triggering contextual questions from user interactions like button clicks and chart selections.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="next-steps"&gt;Next steps&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To put these &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/quick/latest/userguide/brand-customization.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;customizations&lt;/a&gt; into practice, start by &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/business-intelligence/announcing-embedded-chat-in-amazon-quick-suite/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;embedding the Quick chat into your application&lt;/a&gt;. Apply your brand’s color palette, typography, and layout through container CSS and SDK frame options so the chat feels like a native component. Next, create a custom chat agent in the Quick console with persona instructions that define your domain expertise and communication style.&lt;/p&gt;
