&lt;/footer&gt;</content:encoded>
					
		
		
			</item>
		<item>
		<title>How Axonius built secure multi-tenant AI agents on Bedrock AgentCore</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/how-axonius-built-secure-multi-tenant-ai-agents-on-bedrock-agentcore/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Amir Krispin]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Tue, 18 Aug 2026 16:27:07 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Amazon Bedrock]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Amazon Bedrock AgentCore]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Customer Solutions]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">a1f4ace6ed8c5df4ecb9d93eabb3007247912d1f</guid>

					<description>Learn how Axonius, a cybersecurity SaaS provider, used Amazon Bedrock AgentCore to deploy fully isolated, multi-tenant AI agents across hundreds of customer environments, without building custom compute isolation, authentication, or observability infrastructure from scratch.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;Independent Software Vendors (ISVs) are expanding their offerings and adding AI agents. Common considerations for organizations when adding agentic workloads include security, scalability, time to market, and cost tracking. ISVs have another dimension, they provide services to other organizations and need to manage agentic workloads for each customer. As such, ISVs need to manage those common challenges not only broadly but at the tenant level.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;a href="https://www.axonius.com/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Axonius&lt;/a&gt; is the asset intelligence platform that helps Security and IT teams prioritize risks and coordinate fixes. By reconciling data from over 1,400 systems into one authoritative source of truth, Axonius enables security teams and the teams they support to collaborate effectively, reducing the manual burden of security, audit, and compliance by up to 50%. Axonius runs its software as a service (SaaS) infrastructure on AWS, managing hundreds of isolated customer environments.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore is a platform to build, connect, and optimize agents at scale, with any framework or model. In this post, we cover strategies available for SaaS providers to deploy AI agents, describe how Amazon Bedrock AgentCore supports those options, look into Axonius’s considerations, and share Axonius’s chosen architecture. We also describe how Axonius integrated agentic workloads with its existing methodology. This post is for platform engineers and architects building secure, multi-tenant AI agent deployments on AWS.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="multi-tenancy-patterns-for-ai-agents"&gt;Multi-tenancy patterns for AI agents&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Independent Software Vendors (ISVs) tend to provide services using the SaaS model. When choosing an architecture, ISVs have &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/wellarchitected/latest/saas-lens/silo-pool-and-bridge-models.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;three common architectural patterns: silo, bridge, and pool.&lt;/a&gt; The following sections elaborate on what each model means when we look into agentic AI with &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/agentcore/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The &lt;em&gt;silo model&lt;/em&gt; refers to an architecture where tenants are provided dedicated resources. Looking into AgentCore runtime, it means using a dedicated agent per tenant. With the &lt;em&gt;pool model&lt;/em&gt;, tenants share resources, one agent serves multiple tenants. With AgentCore runtim, you can &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/runtime-sessions.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;isolate each user session&lt;/a&gt; by allocating a unique session ID for each user session. The third pattern is the &lt;em&gt;bridge model&lt;/em&gt; in which some components are in silo mode while others are in pool mode. For example, we can have a dedicated agent deployed into AgentCore runtime using shared &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/knowledge-bases/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock Knowledge Bases&lt;/a&gt;, an AWS managed Retrieval Augmented Generation (RAG) service.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="the-challenge"&gt;The challenge&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Axonius was looking to add AI agents to their offering. Their first AI agent interprets the state of large enterprise environments and identifies gaps and risks, using AI to analyze and make sense of millions of data points from dozens of concurrent integration sources. This initiative allows junior analysts to run complex analyses without tying up senior analysts in hours of manual work. Axonius opted to maintain their current tenant management methodology.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Axonius’s SaaS deployment model is silo. Each customer workload resides in a dedicated Amazon Virtual Private Cloud (Amazon VPC). The VPC contains an Application Load Balancer (ALB), Network Load Balancer (NLB), databases, and general compute infrastructure.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Axonius wanted to introduce AI agents while keeping a silo deployment model. Axonius needed to address several key requirements:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Tenant isolation –&lt;/strong&gt;&amp;nbsp;Axonius handles sensitive customer data. An agent serving one customer must be scoped exclusively to that customer’s data.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Identity –&lt;/strong&gt;&amp;nbsp;The existing service has an authentication and authorization module in place, residing on the Amazon Elastic Compute Cloud (Amazon EC2) allocated to the tenant. Axonius needed to integrate the agent’s identity flow with the existing module without disruption.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Cost tracking –&lt;/strong&gt;&amp;nbsp;Agentic costs can spiral. Most of the cost is in model invocation, thus it’s critical to track model cost per tenant. Knowing your cost is essential when considering pricing options for agentic offerings.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Integration with the service&lt;/strong&gt; – The agent instance associated with a tenant needs to have secure access to that tenant workload’s APIs.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Lifecycle management&lt;/strong&gt; – Adding the agentic workload to the current silo Continuous Delivery (CD) workflow.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Observability –&lt;/strong&gt;&amp;nbsp;With a silo model, Axonius will have a large number of agents. The DevOps team needed high quality, straightforward to integrate observability that can track large fleets of agents, issue alarms when things go wrong, and provide tracing capabilities to debug issues.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following sections cover the options Axonius evaluated and what they chose.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="possible-solutions"&gt;Possible solutions&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Looking into tenant deployment modes, Axonius considered the following options.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="option-1-pool-shared-runtime-with-jwt-based-tenant-routing"&gt;Option 1: Pool — shared runtime with JWT-based tenant routing&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;AgentCore runtime enforces structural separation by allocating a dedicated microVM for each session. A single runtime serves tenants while maintaining full tenant separation through application-level controls.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h4 id="how-it-works"&gt;How it works&lt;/h4&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Tenants authenticate through an OAuth 2.0 identity provider (for example, Amazon Cognito) with JWTs carrying a unique tenant claim (for example, custom:tenant_id). The runtime’s &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/inbound-jwt-authorizer.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;built-in JWT authorizer&lt;/a&gt; uses the configured discovery URL to fetch public keys and validate the token’s issuer. The agent code then reads the claim to route tool calls and data access to the correct tenant environment.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div id="attachment_136732" style="width: 4351px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img aria-describedby="caption-attachment-136732" loading="lazy" class="size-full wp-image-136732" src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/06/multi-tanant-gateway.drawio.png" alt="" width="4341" height="1236"&gt;
 &lt;p id="caption-attachment-136732" class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 1: Pool model with a single shared runtime and JWT-based tenant routing&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h4 id="advantages"&gt;Advantages&lt;/h4&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Operational simplicity&lt;/strong&gt;: Only one runtime to deploy, monitor, and update.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Rapid onboarding&lt;/strong&gt;: New customers onboard immediately without infrastructure provisioning, reducing time-to-value.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h4 id="disadvantages"&gt;Disadvantages&lt;/h4&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Application-dependent isolation&lt;/strong&gt;: Tenant separation relies entirely on application code.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Homogeneous deployment&lt;/strong&gt;: Per-tenant customization requires additional conditional logic.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="option-2-bridge-shared-runtime-plus-gateway-enforced-tool-isolation"&gt;Option 2: Bridge — shared runtime plus gateway-enforced tool isolation&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A hybrid approach combines the operational simplicity of a single shared Runtime with infrastructure-level tenant enforcement at the tool layer through AgentCore Gateway. Tenants share one Runtime, but every outbound tool call passes through a Gateway that enforces tenant boundaries before the tool code executes.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h4 id="how-it-works-1"&gt;How it works&lt;/h4&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Tenants connect to the same AgentCore runtime, which uses dedicated microVMs per session for compute isolation, identical to Option 1. The difference is that every tool invocation routes through a shared AgentCore Gateway with two enforcement mechanisms:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Policy in Amazon Bedrock AgentCore&lt;/strong&gt; – Deterministic access control. Cedar rules evaluate each tool call against the caller’s identity attributes (for example, Cognito group claims) and produce an allow/deny decision. A forbid rule can block specific tenant groups from invoking restricted tools.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;AWS Lambda Interceptor (REQUEST)&lt;/strong&gt; – Dynamic validation and context enrichment. Runs before the tool call reaches the target extracts the JWT, looks up tenant context, and exchanges the token for short-lived, tenant-scoped IAM credentials through STS AssumeRole (“act-on-behalf” pattern). The downstream tool receives these least-privilege credentials instead of the original JWT.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The Gateway evaluates the interceptor &lt;em&gt;before&lt;/em&gt; the Cedar policy, enabling the interceptor to enrich the request context that the policy then evaluates.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A RESPONSE interceptor can additionally filter tool discovery based on tenant identity.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This means two independent mechanisms enforce tenant isolation at the infrastructure layer, outside agent code.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div id="attachment_136733" style="width: 3397px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img aria-describedby="caption-attachment-136733" loading="lazy" class="size-full wp-image-136733" src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/06/multi-tanant-slio.drawio.png" alt="" width="3387" height="1236"&gt;
 &lt;p id="caption-attachment-136733" class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 2: Bridge model with a shared runtime and gateway-enforced tool isolation&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h4 id="advantages-1"&gt;Advantages&lt;/h4&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Layered infrastructure enforcement:&lt;/strong&gt; Even if the agent code has a routing bug, the Gateway blocks cross-tenant tool calls. Cedar policy (deterministic) and a Lambda interceptor (dynamic) provide defense in depth.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Centralized governance&lt;/strong&gt;: A single Gateway to audit and enforce tenant policies across all tool invocations, with every decision logged to Amazon CloudWatch.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Shared efficiency:&lt;/strong&gt; Runtime, knowledge bases, and observability infrastructure are managed centrally without sharing security risk.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h4 id="disadvantages-1"&gt;Disadvantages&lt;/h4&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Setup complexity:&lt;/strong&gt; Requires configuring a Gateway, REQUEST/RESPONSE interceptor Lambdas, tenant mappings, Cedar policies, and STS role trust relationships. This adds significantly more moving parts than Option 1 or 3.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;VPC connectivity&lt;/strong&gt;: Backend tools typically reside in a VPC, requiring VPC endpoints for private Gateway connectivity, which adds networking complexity and potential points of failure.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="option-3-silo-dedicated-runtime-per-tenant"&gt;Option 3: Silo — dedicated runtime per tenant&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In the silo model, every tenant operates on a dedicated AgentCore runtime. Access control is enforced entirely through AWS Identity and Access Management (IAM). Resource-based policies on both the Runtime and its endpoint determine which principals, whether same-account roles or cross-account identities, are allowed to invoke the agent. Because each tenant’s workload runs on separate infrastructure, there is no shared compute between tenants.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div id="attachment_136735" style="width: 3610px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img aria-describedby="caption-attachment-136735" loading="lazy" class="size-full wp-image-136735" src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/06/multi-tantns-microVM-session.drawio.png" alt="" width="3600" height="936"&gt;
 &lt;p id="caption-attachment-136735" class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 3: Silo model with a dedicated runtime per tenant&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h4 id="how-it-works-2"&gt;How it works&lt;/h4&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Each tenant gets a dedicated AgentCore runtime, provisioned automatically (for example, with AWS CloudFormation or CDK) when a new customer is onboarded. Within that runtime, each user session runs in its own isolated microVM, so no process state is shared even between users of the same tenant. Access control is enforced through IAM resource-based policies attached to both the AgentCore runtime and its endpoint. The caller must hold explicit permission for their tenant’s specific runtime.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h4 id="advantages-2"&gt;Advantages&lt;/h4&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Maximum isolation:&lt;/strong&gt; Dedicated compute per tenant with no shared process state. Each session runs in its own microVM, and tenants cannot reach each other’s infrastructure.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Simple authorization model:&lt;/strong&gt; IAM resource-based policies, applied to both the Runtime and its endpoint, are the sole enforcement point. No application-level routing logic required.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Independent configuration:&lt;/strong&gt; Each runtime can run a different agent version, model, or endpoint configuration without affecting other tenants.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h4 id="disadvantages-2"&gt;Disadvantages&lt;/h4&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Scale limitations:&lt;/strong&gt; The default quota is 1,000 agents per AWS account (adjustable through Service Quotas), which requires capacity planning for large customer bases.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Provisioning latency:&lt;/strong&gt; Each new tenant requires creating a dedicated Runtime and endpoint, introducing onboarding delays compared to a shared-runtime model.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Operational overhead:&lt;/strong&gt; Monitoring, updating, and managing hundreds of runtimes adds operational complexity that demands robust automation (for example, CDK/CloudFormation pipelines, centralized observability).&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="solution-overview"&gt;Solution overview&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Axonius operates today in a siloed mode and chooses to continue with that approach when adding agents. Each customer has a dedicated agent. Axonius designed a multi-tenant agentic AI architecture using Amazon Bedrock and AgentCore with the following key components:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;AgentCore runtime&lt;/strong&gt; – Axonius deploys a dedicated agent for each customer, and each user session runs on an isolated microVM, using AgentCore runtime.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Amazon Elastic Container Registry (Amazon ECR)&lt;/strong&gt; – Stores per-tenant agent container images.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Amazon Bedrock&lt;/strong&gt; – Powers the underlying foundation models (FMs), with IAM role tagging for cost allocation.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Amazon Bedrock Knowledge Bases (KB)&lt;/strong&gt; – Axonius uses Amazon Bedrock Knowledge Bases (KB) with Amazon S3 Vectors because of their cost efficiency and ability to scale. Axonius uses metadata filtering to isolate tenant-specific data.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Amazon Bedrock Guardrails&lt;/strong&gt; – Provides content filtering and topic denial policies. Guardrails are applied to every model response, keeping responses safe and in scope before they are shared with the user.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Amazon CloudWatch&lt;/strong&gt; – Monitors the overall workload and is instrumental in cost control. It tracks token consumption metrics (input/output), issuing alerts and using IAM-deny enforcement for cost governance. If a customer exceeds their token budget, an automated IAM policy blocks further invocations.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Amazon VPC Lattice&lt;/strong&gt; –&amp;nbsp;Allows Axonius to manage cost-efficient private connectivity, connecting AgentCore runtime, customer VPCs, and AWS service endpoints.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The architecture uses AWS CloudFormation for automated per-customer provisioning and teardown, allowing Axonius to scale agent deployments across their customer base.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div id="attachment_137132" style="width: 2570px" class="wp-caption alignleft"&gt;
 &lt;img aria-describedby="caption-attachment-137132" loading="lazy" class="size-full wp-image-137132" src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/11/AgentCore-HLD-Copy-of-AgentCore.jpeg" alt="" width="2560" height="2089"&gt;
 &lt;p id="caption-attachment-137132" class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
 &lt;p&gt;Figure 4: Axonius multi-tenant agentic architecture on Amazon Bedrock AgentCore&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="why-amazon-bedrock-agentcore-runtime"&gt;Why Amazon Bedrock AgentCore runtime?&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;After evaluating multiple approaches, including running agents as additional containers within their existing EC2 instances, Axonius chose Amazon Bedrock AgentCore runtime. Its purpose-built capabilities directly addressed their multi-tenant SaaS requirements.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Session isolation with microVMs&lt;/strong&gt; – AgentCore runtime’s session isolation was the decisive factor. Each user session runs in a &lt;em&gt;dedicated microVM&lt;/em&gt; with isolated CPU, memory, and filesystem resources. After session completion, the entire microVM is terminated and memory is sanitized. This deterministic security model was critical for Axonius, where each customer’s data, including sensitive cybersecurity asset inventories, must remain completely isolated.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Framework flexibility and VPC integration&lt;/strong&gt; – AgentCore runtime’s framework-agnostic design allowed Axonius to deploy agents using their preferred tooling while connecting securely to their existing VPC infrastructure. Each agent connects through an ENI to the customer’s VPC, allowing direct interaction with the Axonius application APIs running inside the isolated EC2 instances.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Built-in observability&lt;/strong&gt; – The built-in observability capabilities gave Axonius the visibility they needed without building custom monitoring infrastructure. These capabilities include CloudWatch integration for logging, AWS X-Ray for distributed tracing, and agent-specific tracing that captures reasoning steps and tool invocations.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="user-session-flow"&gt;User session flow&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Each customer runs their own Axonius deployment on a dedicated workload inside their own VPC. Every customer also gets a dedicated AgentCore runtime: a per-customer agent that reasons with Claude, pulls product knowledge from an Amazon Bedrock Knowledge Base, and reaches back into that customer’s Axonius API to answer data questions. Customers have a chat box that allows them to write questions. The following flow traces a customer question, for example, &lt;em&gt;“&lt;em&gt;Are there any major changes in my asset count compared to last week?&lt;/em&gt;”&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Authentication:&lt;/strong&gt; The user is already signed in to Axonius on their own instance. When they send a chat message, the Axonius application authenticates the request and mints a short-lived impersonation JWT carrying the user’s identity, tenant ID, session ID, and actor ID. This token, rather than a static credential, authorizes everything downstream.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Runtime invocation&lt;/strong&gt;: The Axonius application control plane assembles the invocation payload and calls InvokeAgentRuntime on the customer’s dedicated AgentCore runtime. The payload carries only tenant configuration: the AgentCore Memory ID, Knowledge Base and data-source IDs, AWS Region, current Axonius version, and the callback address for the customer’s own instance. The JWT rides in a custom AgentCore header rather than in the body, so authentication material does not land in the agent’s saved state, and the session and actor IDs scope the call to one user’s conversation.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Dedicated, isolated runtime&lt;/strong&gt;: Each customer has its own AgentCore runtime, and that runtime is attached to a dedicated elastic network interface (ENI) placed inside the customer’s own VPC and subnet. AgentCore creates an isolated session, validates the JWT back against the customer’s Axonius application (signature, expiry, revocation, and permissions), and scopes the reasoning to that single tenant. An invalid token is rejected before the model runs.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Agent reasoning with Claude&lt;/strong&gt;: The team’s existing LangGraph supervisor inspects the question and routes it to the right specialist agent. The specialist uses Amazon Bedrock to analyze the question, deciding at each step whether it can respond directly, needs product documentation, or needs live data from the customer’s environment. For model availability by Region, refer to &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/models-regions.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Supported models by AWS Region&lt;/a&gt; in Amazon Bedrock.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Knowledge retrieval:&lt;/strong&gt; When the question is about how Axonius works, the agent retrieves relevant passages from a Bedrock Knowledge Base. That knowledge base is kept current by uploading the latest Axonius documentation to Amazon Simple Storage Service (Amazon S3) and syncing it, so responses reflect the customer’s running version.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Tool access into the customer’s Axonius API&lt;/strong&gt;: When the question needs live data, such as “Give me a high-level summary of all assets discovered in the last 24 hours,” the agent invokes its query tool, which: 
  &lt;ol type="a"&gt; 
   &lt;li&gt;Uses the Knowledge Base and large language model (LLM) to translate the natural-language question into an Axonius Query Language (AQL) expression.&lt;/li&gt; 
   &lt;li&gt;Reaches the customer’s Axonius application instance through the runtime’s dedicated ENI, so the traffic stays inside the customer’s VPC and doesn’t leave the AWS network. It compiles the query and fetches the matching assets, carrying the same JWT, so the agent can only ever see data the user is already allowed to see.&lt;/li&gt; 
   &lt;li&gt;Returns the results to the agent, which reasons over them to compose the response.&lt;/li&gt; 
  &lt;/ol&gt; &lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Guardrails and response&lt;/strong&gt;: Amazon Bedrock Guardrails are applied server-side to every model response, keeping responses safe and on scope before they leave Amazon Bedrock. The agent’s answer streams back token by token through the runtime to the chat UI, and the exchange is persisted in AgentCore Memory so follow-up questions keep context.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="tenant-isolation-and-private-networking"&gt;Tenant isolation and private networking&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The whole design rests on strict per-customer isolation and private connectivity:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Dedicated runtime per customer:&lt;/strong&gt; Every customer gets its own AgentCore runtime with its own ENI inside their VPC and subnet, so one customer’s agent has no network path to another customer’s data.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;In-network access to customer data:&lt;/strong&gt; Because the ENI lives in the customer’s VPC, the runtime reaches the internal Axonius EC2 instance over private addressing. That traffic doesn’t traverse the public internet and does not leave the AWS network.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Shared AWS services over Amazon VPC Lattice:&lt;/strong&gt; A dedicated Axonius services VPC publishes the AWS services the runtime depends on (Amazon ECR, Amazon S3, Amazon Bedrock, and others) through a set of private endpoints, shared with each customer VPC through VPC Lattice. This lets the AgentCore runtime pull the agent container image from Amazon ECR and reach Amazon Bedrock and the Knowledge Base privately, while restricting access so that only the customer VPC and its runtime can reach those services.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Networking cost efficiency:&lt;/strong&gt; Amazon VPC Lattice allows Axonius to define one private endpoint per AWS service, reducing cost and management overhead. With AWS PrivateLink, Axonius would have needed to define a private endpoint per VPC per service.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Together, this keeps a clean trust boundary: the reasoning, knowledge retrieval, and guardrails run on Amazon Bedrock. The authoritative data and identity stay on each customer’s own VPC. And every hop between them travels private AWS networking. The agent does not hold long-lived credentials. It borrows the user’s JWT for the life of a request, and every session, memory, tool call, and network path is scoped to a single tenant.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="token-governance-and-cost-management"&gt;Token governance and cost management&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A notable aspect of Axonius’s implementation is their approach to token governance:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;CloudWatch metrics&lt;/strong&gt; track sum of input and output tokens per agent. In addition, Axonius uses opentelemetry-instrument to get real-time token usage.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;IAM role tagging&lt;/strong&gt; (using Amazon Bedrock cost allocation by IAM user/role feature) allows per-tenant cost attribution.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Automated IAM-deny policies&lt;/strong&gt; can be triggered by using CloudWatch alarms to block runaway agent usage.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Application Inference Profiles&lt;/strong&gt; per model allow granular tagging, alerting, and cost control through Amazon EventBridge.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This approach provides cost allocation visibility updated once or twice daily, with real-time alerting through CloudWatch for immediate enforcement.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;ISVs are in the process of developing agentic AI workloads. This post describes possible avenues and Axonius’s chosen solution, using Amazon Bedrock and AgentCore as the foundation for its agentic AI offering.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;By using the AgentCore managed runtime, Knowledge Bases for RAG, and built-in session memory, Axonius reduced the development cycle for their multi-tenant AI agent from an estimated eight weeks of custom infrastructure work to only 10 days of production-ready deployment, a 75 percent reduction in time-to-market.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;blockquote&gt;
 &lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;“AgentCore Runtime gave us the multi-tenant isolation and authentication framework we needed to deploy AI agents across large numbers of customer environments without compromising our security-first architecture.”&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/blockquote&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;— Dori Shmuel, SaaS DevOps Lead, Axonius&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To learn more about building multi-tenant AI agents with Amazon Bedrock AgentCore runtime, we recommend you review these additional resources:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/agents-tools-runtime.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore runtime documentation&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://catalog.us-east-1.prod.workshops.aws/workshops/749d2432-98e2-4af5-b8d8-7242395c1925/en-US" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Getting Started with Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Workshop&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/observability-configure.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AgentCore Observability Configuration&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt;

		<title>NVIDIA Nemotron 3.5 Lightning now available in Amazon SageMaker JumpStart</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/nvidia-nemotron-3-5-lightning-now-available-in-amazon-sagemaker-jumpstart/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Venu Kanamatareddy]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Mon, 17 Aug 2026 18:06:33 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Advanced (300)]]></category>

					<description>NVIDIA Nemotron 3.5 Lightning, an open model built for high-volume agentic workloads, is now available in Amazon SageMaker JumpStart. This post shows how to deploy the 30B Mixture-of-Experts model (3B active), which delivers up to 4x higher throughput and up to 30% faster task completion for always-on agents.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;NVIDIA Nemotron 3.5 Lightning is designed for the fast, specialized model execution required by high-volume agentic workloads. With NVIDIA Nemotron 3.5 Lightning on Amazon SageMaker JumpStart, you can access an open model designed for high-volume agentic workloads.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;With this launch, you can deploy Nemotron 3.5 Lightning from &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/ai/jumpstart/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon SageMaker JumpStart&lt;/a&gt; without configuring the serving infrastructure yourself. NVIDIA describes Lightning as the fastest open model in its class for powering always-on agents. It delivers up to 4x higher throughput and up to 30% faster task completion on high-volume agentic workloads. At 30B total parameters with only 3B active, it can run on a single supported GPU. Repetitive, specialized steps in agent workflows can therefore run without frontier-scale infrastructure. In this post, we show you how to deploy Nemotron 3.5 Lightning from SageMaker JumpStart.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="overview-of-nvidia-nemotron-3.5-lightning"&gt;Overview of NVIDIA Nemotron 3.5 Lightning&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Nemotron 3.5 Lightning is a publicly available foundation model distilled from NVIDIA’s frontier Nemotron 3 Ultra and developed with the Nemotron Coalition. It uses a hybrid Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) architecture and is trained specifically for agentic tool use across popular agent harnesses. It is trained on open datasets and released as an open model, so you can customize it, own the resulting weights, and deploy it wherever your agents run.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following table summarizes the key specifications and performance characteristics of NVIDIA Nemotron 3.5 Lightning.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Specification&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Details&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Architecture&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Hybrid Mixture-of-Experts (MoE)&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Parameters&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;30B total / 3B active&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Context length&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Up to 1M tokens&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Input / Output&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Text in, text out&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Speculative decoding&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;DFlash&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Throughput&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Up to 4x higher for high-volume agentic workloads&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Task completion&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Up to 30% faster&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Distilled from&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;NVIDIA Nemotron 3 Ultra&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="not-every-agent-step-needs-a-frontier-model"&gt;Not every agent step needs a frontier model&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Always-on agents work continuously: they gather context, observe their environment, reason over what they know, and act. Many of these steps can involve model calls, but they do not all require the same level of capability. Planning a multi-stage workflow or orchestrating sub-agents can demand frontier-level reasoning. Classifying an alert, extracting fields from a form, or checking a record against a policy can often be handled by a smaller, specialized model. These tasks can account for a large share of call volume.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In many cases, running all model-backed steps through a single large model can add frontier-model cost and latency to work that a smaller, specialized model can handle. A system-of-models approach can instead route each step to a model suited to the task.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Nemotron 3.5 Lightning is built for the high-volume end of that system. Its MoE architecture activates 3B of 30B parameters per forward pass, helping maintain high throughput across long, multi-turn sessions. DFlash speculative decoding can further reduce per-token latency. The 1M-token context window allows an agent to carry accumulated state across a long-running session without repeated re-grounding.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;If &lt;a href="https://blogs.nvidia.com/blog/nemotron-lightning-switchyard-rtx-dgx/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;NVIDIA NeMo Switchyard&lt;/a&gt; is part of your stack, it can route individual workflow steps across your chosen model pool. Lightning can be selected for high-volume specialized steps where its speed and domain-specific accuracy are a good fit.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="accuracy-across-reasoning-and-agentic-benchmarks"&gt;Accuracy across reasoning and agentic benchmarks&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Across the published evaluations, NVFP4 remains close to BF16 on many tasks, as summarized in the following table. NVIDIA reports that the evaluation recipes and commands used to produce these results are published in &lt;a href="https://docs.nvidia.com/nemo/gym/about/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;NeMo Gym&lt;/a&gt;. Accuracy results were measured by NVIDIA under a consistent harness and may differ from vendors’ self-reported numbers.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following table compares the BF16 and NVFP4 variants of Nemotron 3.5 Lightning across key reasoning and agentic benchmarks.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Benchmark&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;BF16&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;NVFP4&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt;

  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt;

  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt;

  &lt;/tr&gt;

  &lt;/tr&gt;

   &lt;td&gt;72.88&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;AA-LCR&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;52.00&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;49.19&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="customizing-for-domain-accuracy"&gt;Customizing for domain accuracy&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Organizations can post-train the model with NVIDIA NeMo for domain-specific tools, workflows, and policies, then deploy the resulting model in their chosen environment. The SageMaker JumpStart model card for this launch does not expose JumpStart customization.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="enterprise-use-cases"&gt;Enterprise use cases&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Lightning is built for the specialized, high-frequency work inside agent workflows:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Personal agents&lt;/strong&gt;: Long-running assistants handling email, calendar, projects, and bookings, with the option to run locally for contextual data.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Financial services&lt;/strong&gt;: Extracting data from documents, checking policy rules, monitoring risk signals, and preparing structured summaries.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Cyber security operations&lt;/strong&gt;: Enriching alerts, classifying incidents, querying logs, validating controls, correlating indicators, and preparing findings for analysts.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Telecom&lt;/strong&gt;: Triaging network alarms, optimizing network configurations, and answering billing questions.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Retail&lt;/strong&gt;: Enriching product catalogs, resolving inventory and fulfillment exceptions, assisting product discovery, and handling order, return, and loyalty questions.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="getting-started-with-sagemaker-jumpstart"&gt;Getting started with SageMaker JumpStart&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;You can deploy Nemotron 3.5 Lightning through Amazon SageMaker JumpStart without manually configuring the serving framework.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="prerequisites"&gt;Prerequisites&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Before you begin, make sure you have:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;An AWS account.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Appropriately scoped permissions for SageMaker JumpStart.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Sufficient service quota for GPU instances (for example, &lt;code&gt;ml.g6e.12xlarge&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;ml.p4d.24xlarge&lt;/code&gt;, or &lt;code&gt;ml.p5.48xlarge&lt;/code&gt;).&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Important:&lt;/strong&gt; Deploying this model creates a SageMaker AI endpoint that incurs charges while running. For details, see &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/ai/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon SageMaker AI pricing&lt;/a&gt;. Delete your endpoint when finished to avoid ongoing charges.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="deploy-using-sagemaker-studio"&gt;Deploy using SageMaker Studio&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Open Amazon SageMaker Studio.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;In the navigation pane, choose &lt;strong&gt;SageMaker JumpStart&lt;/strong&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Search for Nemotron 3.5 Lightning. &lt;p&gt;&lt;/p&gt;
  &lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
   &lt;img loading="lazy" src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/14/ML-21710-1.png" alt="SageMaker JumpStart search results listing the Nemotron 3.5 Lightning model" width="800" height="518"&gt;
   &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 1: Searching for Nemotron 3.5 Lightning in SageMaker JumpStart&lt;/p&gt;
  &lt;/div&gt;&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Select the model card. The NVFP4 model ID is &lt;code&gt;huggingface-reasoning-nemotron-3-5-lightning-30b-a3b-nvfp4&lt;/code&gt;. For BF16, use &lt;code&gt;huggingface-reasoning-nemotron-3-5-lightning-30b-a3b-bf16&lt;/code&gt;. 
  &lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
   &lt;img loading="lazy" src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/14/ML-21710-2.png" alt="Nemotron 3.5 Lightning model card in SageMaker JumpStart showing the NVFP4 model ID" width="800" height="804"&gt;
   &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 2: The Nemotron 3.5 Lightning model card in SageMaker JumpStart&lt;/p&gt;
  &lt;/div&gt; &lt;p&gt;&lt;/p&gt;
  &lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
   &lt;img loading="lazy" src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/14/ML-21710-3.png" alt="Additional Nemotron 3.5 Lightning model card details in SageMaker JumpStart" width="800" height="795"&gt;
   &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 3: Model card details for Nemotron 3.5 Lightning&lt;/p&gt;
  &lt;/div&gt;&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Choose &lt;strong&gt;Deploy&lt;/strong&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Select your instance type (for example, &lt;code&gt;ml.g6e.24xlarge&lt;/code&gt;). &lt;p&gt;&lt;/p&gt;
  &lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
   &lt;img loading="lazy" src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/14/ML-21710-4.png" alt="SageMaker JumpStart deployment settings with an instance type selected" width="800" height="581"&gt;
   &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 4: Selecting an instance type for deployment&lt;/p&gt;
  &lt;/div&gt;&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Review the deployment settings. The defaults are sufficient for most use cases.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Choose &lt;strong&gt;Deploy&lt;/strong&gt; to create the endpoint.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Wait for the endpoint status to show InService before running inference. &lt;p&gt;&lt;/p&gt;
  &lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
   &lt;img loading="lazy" src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/14/ML-21710-5.png" alt="SageMaker endpoint status page showing the endpoint InService" width="800" height="563"&gt;
   &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 5: Endpoint status showing InService&lt;/p&gt;
  &lt;/div&gt;&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="deploy-from-hugging-face"&gt;Deploy from Hugging Face&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;You can also deploy NVIDIA Nemotron 3.5 Lightning to Amazon SageMaker AI from its Hugging Face model page. On the Hugging Face model page, choose &lt;strong&gt;Deploy&lt;/strong&gt;, select &lt;strong&gt;Amazon SageMaker AI&lt;/strong&gt;, and then choose &lt;strong&gt;Deploy on SageMaker AI&lt;/strong&gt;. This opens the SageMaker AI deployment workflow, where you can configure and deploy the model.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/14/ML-21710-6.png" alt="Hugging Face model page with the Amazon SageMaker AI deployment option selected" width="800"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;Figure 6: Deploying from the Hugging Face model page&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="deploy-using-the-sagemaker-python-sdk"&gt;Deploy using the SageMaker Python SDK&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;With SageMaker JumpStart, you can access the NVFP4 and BF16 variants. The following example uses the NVFP4 model ID &lt;code&gt;huggingface-reasoning-nemotron-3-5-lightning-30b-a3b-nvfp4&lt;/code&gt;. For BF16, use &lt;code&gt;huggingface-reasoning-nemotron-3-5-lightning-30b-a3b-bf16&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;from sagemaker.jumpstart.model import JumpStartModel

model_id = "huggingface-reasoning-nemotron-3-5-lightning-30b-a3b-nvfp4"
model_version = "*"

model = JumpStartModel(model_id=model_id, model_version=model_version)
predictor = model.deploy()&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="clean-up"&gt;Clean up&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To avoid unnecessary charges, delete the SageMaker AI endpoint when you are done:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;predictor.delete_endpoint()&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;NVIDIA Nemotron 3.5 Lightning brings fast, specialized agent execution to Amazon SageMaker JumpStart. NVIDIA reports up to 4x higher throughput and up to 30% faster task completion for high-volume specialized work. Its hybrid MoE architecture, 3B active parameters, DFlash speculative decoding, and 1M-token context are designed for high-volume agent workflows. Because the model is open and designed for customization, you can post-train it for your own tools and policies and retain control of the resulting model.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Nemotron 3.5 Lightning can support specialized agent workloads across personal assistants, financial services, security operations, telecom, and retail. You can deploy it today from SageMaker JumpStart.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Get started by searching for Nemotron 3.5 Lightning in &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/ai/jumpstart/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon SageMaker JumpStart&lt;/a&gt;. For deployment guidance, see &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/dg/jumpstart-foundation-models-use.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;JumpStart foundation model usage&lt;/a&gt; in the Amazon SageMaker AI Developer Guide.&lt;/p&gt;

			</item>
		<item>
		<title>Build OpenClaw agents that transact with Amazon Bedrock AgentCore payments</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/build-openclaw-agents-that-transact-with-amazon-bedrock-agentcore-payments/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Daniel Wirjo]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Mon, 17 Aug 2026 16:19:56 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Advanced (300)]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Amazon Bedrock AgentCore]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">09559dd685a47bca5e3b94a074ceb00bc15d872e</guid>

					<description>Give an autonomous agent a wallet and spending guardrails so it can pay for paywalled APIs, MCP servers, and web content. This post connects OpenClaw to Amazon Bedrock AgentCore payments and the x402 protocol, using the aws-agents-pay plugin to make bounded, human-approved testnet payments.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;This post is a collaboration between AWS and the &lt;a href="https://www.openclaw.org/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;OpenClaw Foundation&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Autonomous agents that browse the web, call APIs, and query Model Context Protocol (MCP) servers can encounter services that require an HTTP 402 Payment Required response to be settled before access continues. To use those services without pausing for a human at every transaction, an agent needs a payment path that operates within limits approved in advance. A safe design keeps wallet-provider credentials and the authority to create or expand payment sessions outside the model-facing runtime. The runtime can still initiate approved payments within those limits.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/payments.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AgentCore payments&lt;/a&gt;, a capability of &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/agentcore/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore&lt;/a&gt;, provides wallet integration, spending limits, and a consistent payment layer as agent payment protocols evolve. Protocols such as &lt;a href="https://github.com/coinbase/x402" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;x402&lt;/a&gt; and &lt;a href="https://mpp.dev/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Machine Payments Protocol (MPP)&lt;/a&gt; support programmatic payment flows between agents and services. In this post, you connect OpenClaw to a wallet and bounded payment session that a human provisions through a trusted administrative path, then use the &lt;code&gt;aws-agents-pay&lt;/code&gt; plugin for &lt;a href="https://openclaw.ai" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;OpenClaw&lt;/a&gt; to initiate approved testnet payments.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="why-agents-need-payment-capabilities"&gt;Why agents need payment capabilities&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Autonomous agents can act across multiple steps and services without a human reviewing each action. A long-running research or workflow agent might encounter a paid API or content endpoint while no operator is present. A bounded payment layer lets the agent continue within recipient, asset, network, per-payment, cumulative budget, and expiry limits that a human sets in advance.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Some APIs, content services, compute services, and MCP tools use pay-per-use pricing. Individual transactions can be less than one dollar or fractions of a cent. Minimum card-processing fees can make transactions at that scale cost-prohibitive, while stablecoin payments can support small amounts and near-real-time settlement. This makes HTTP-native protocols such as x402 suitable for programmatic, agent-initiated payments.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Implementing this directly requires wallet-provider integration, protected credential handling, payment authorization, deterministic limits, and payment observability. You also need to account for a model that can be manipulated by untrusted input. The design does not prevent prompt injection. Instead, it assumes untrusted input can manipulate the model and bounds the runtime’s authority by recipient, asset, network, per-payment amount, session budget, and expiry.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="solution-overview"&gt;Solution overview&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;AgentCore payments provides a consistent payment layer as agent payment protocols evolve. This OpenClaw integration demonstrates the pattern using x402 v2. It processes a merchant’s HTTP 402 challenge through a configured wallet provider and returns a signed authorization that the client uses to replay the request. &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/identity.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AgentCore Identity, a capability of Amazon Bedrock AgentCore,&lt;/a&gt; stores wallet-provider credentials. &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/observability.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AgentCore Observability, a capability of Amazon Bedrock AgentCore,&lt;/a&gt; can provide logs, metrics, and traces through Amazon CloudWatch and AWS X-Ray when telemetry delivery is configured.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;AgentCore payments supports a Coinbase wallet or a Stripe Privy wallet as the payment connection. Both provide an embedded stablecoin wallet. Subject to provider and geographic availability, you or your end users can fund the wallet through stablecoin or fiat using a debit card. Both providers use AgentCore Identity credential storage and per-session spending limits.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This walkthrough uses &lt;a href="https://openclaw.ai/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;OpenClaw&lt;/a&gt;, an AI assistant that runs on your devices and connects models, tools, and messaging channels through a local Gateway. The &lt;a href="https://www.openclaw.org/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;OpenClaw Foundation&lt;/a&gt; maintains the project, and plugins extend what the assistant can do. The walkthrough separates human-run payment administration from the model-facing runtime. A human provisions the wallet, creates the payment session, approves recipients, and sets the budget in a trusted terminal. The &lt;code&gt;aws-agents-pay&lt;/code&gt; plugin exposes two model-visible tools: &lt;code&gt;get_payment_session_status&lt;/code&gt;, which checks the configured payment session, and &lt;code&gt;get_paid_content&lt;/code&gt;, which requests an approved paid URL and completes the payment within the configured policy. The runtime can initiate a payment within the approved session, but it cannot create, extend, or replace a session.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 640px;" class="wp-video"&gt;
 &lt;video class="wp-video-shortcode" id="video-137078-2" width="640" height="360" preload="metadata" controls="controls"&gt;
  &lt;source type="video/mp4" src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/artifacts/DBSBlogs/ml-21616/ml-21616-Weather.mp4?_=2"&gt;
 &lt;/video&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;Figure 1: The demo shows a chat with an AI assistant named “Bob” in the OpenClaw gateway application. The user asks “What’s the weather in Seattle right now?” then Bob executes a test payment of 0.001 USDC on Base Sepolia for a paid weather API&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A typical payment flow is:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;The agent calls &lt;code&gt;get_payment_session_status&lt;/code&gt; to confirm that an operator-provisioned session is usable.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;The agent calls &lt;code&gt;get_paid_content&lt;/code&gt; for an approved URL. The plugin first performs a bounded network probe and receives an HTTP 402 response containing an x402 v2 challenge.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;After receiving the challenge, the plugin verifies that the challenge resource has the same origin and path as the requested URL. It then validates the network, asset, recipient, and amount against the operator’s policy before it calls &lt;code&gt;ProcessPayment&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;For a retry of the same payment request, the plugin reuses the same idempotency token rather than creating a second payment. Concurrent duplicate requests can still present a race, so avoid issuing the same payment concurrently.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;The plugin waits until the signed authorization’s &lt;code&gt;validAfter&lt;/code&gt; time, then replays the original request with the payment signature.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;With &lt;code&gt;returnBody&lt;/code&gt; enabled for this walkthrough, the plugin returns the paid response body, caps it at 10 KiB, and marks it as &lt;code&gt;untrusted: true&lt;/code&gt;. The plugin does not return the signed payment proof to the model.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The current OpenClaw setup flow in the Agent Toolkit for AWS provides an example for Base Sepolia for testing and Base for production, and supports customization for other chains, including Ethereum, other EVM-compatible chains, and Solana.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="collaboration-with-openclaw"&gt;Collaboration with OpenClaw&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;AWS has been collaborating with the OpenClaw Foundation on this integration, including support for community events like &lt;a href="https://www.claw-con.com/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;ClawCon&lt;/a&gt;, and worked directly with the Foundation’s engineering team to bring AgentCore payments into the plugin framework.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;blockquote&gt;
 &lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;“Payments are a natural extension of what plugins already do in OpenClaw: give an agent a new capability through a well-defined tool, not a special case bolted on afterward. We’re grateful for AWS’s support of the OpenClaw ecosystem, from events like ClawCon to working directly with our team on this integration. Agents now get a production-grade path to pay for what they use, while builders keep the same conversational setup they expect from any other OpenClaw plugin. We’re looking forward to going deeper with AWS AgentCore as agentic payments become a bigger part of how agents operate on their own.”&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/blockquote&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;— Patrick Erichsen, Member of Technical Staff, OpenClaw Foundation&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="implementation-steps-configure-openclaw-to-make-bounded-payments"&gt;Implementation steps: Configure OpenClaw to make bounded payments&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following sections walk you through the prerequisites, provisioning the wallet and payment session outside the agent loop, configuring an explicit payment policy, and making a bounded payment.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="prerequisites"&gt;Prerequisites&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Make sure that you have the following prerequisites.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;OpenClaw version 2026.3.24 or later: &lt;code&gt;npm install -g openclaw&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Node.js and npm.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;An AWS account with access to AgentCore payments.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Separate AWS Identity and Access Management (IAM) roles for administration and runtime, following the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/payments-iam-roles.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AgentCore payments IAM roles guide&lt;/a&gt;. The OpenClaw runtime role needs only the read operations required for status plus &lt;code&gt;ProcessPayment&lt;/code&gt;. It must not have session-write permissions.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Coinbase CDP or Stripe with Privy credentials, entered only in the interactive AgentCore CLI.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;A Base Sepolia x402 v2 endpoint that you control or have independently approved. You can use the &lt;a href="https://docs.cdp.coinbase.com/x402/buyer/discover-services" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Coinbase x402 Bazaar&lt;/a&gt; to discover candidate services, but verify the endpoint, recipient, network, asset, and price before adding them to the payment policy.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;The merchant recipient address and USDC asset contract verified from merchant documentation or another known-good source, independently of the HTTP 402 response.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Disclaimer:&lt;/strong&gt; Security for this solution follows the &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/compliance/shared-responsibility-model/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS shared responsibility model&lt;/a&gt;. AWS is responsible for security of the cloud, while you’re responsible for security in the cloud. For this solution, your responsibilities include configuring IAM permissions, protecting wallet-provider credentials, and defining payment policies. Follow the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/payments-iam-roles.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AgentCore payments IAM roles guidance&lt;/a&gt; to separate administrative, management, agent execution, and service operations.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following steps take you from installing the plugin to watching an agent pay for its first piece of paywalled content.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="install-the-plugin-from-clawhub"&gt;1. Install the plugin from ClawHub&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Before continuing, confirm that the &lt;code&gt;@aws/aws-agents-pay&lt;/code&gt; package is available on ClawHub. Then install it:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;div class="code-toolbar"&gt; 
  &lt;pre class="language-bash"&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;openclaw plugins &lt;span class="token function"&gt;install&lt;/span&gt; clawhub:@aws/aws-agents-pay&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
  &lt;div class="toolbar"&gt; 
   &lt;div class="toolbar-item"&gt;
    Bash
   &lt;/div&gt; 
  &lt;/div&gt; 
 &lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The package name is &lt;code&gt;@aws/aws-agents-pay&lt;/code&gt;, the installed plugin ID is &lt;code&gt;aws-agents-pay&lt;/code&gt;, and the bundled skill name is &lt;code&gt;agents-pay&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To inspect the bundled setup guidance, run:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;div class="code-toolbar"&gt; 
  &lt;pre class="language-bash"&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;openclaw skills info agents-pay&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
  &lt;div class="toolbar"&gt; 
   &lt;div class="toolbar-item"&gt;
    Bash
   &lt;/div&gt; 
  &lt;/div&gt; 
 &lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Inspect the installed plugin:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;div class="code-toolbar"&gt; 
  &lt;pre class="language-bash"&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;openclaw plugins inspect aws-agents-pay&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
  &lt;div class="toolbar"&gt; 
   &lt;div class="toolbar-item"&gt;
    Bash
   &lt;/div&gt; 
  &lt;/div&gt; 
 &lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The model-visible runtime must expose only &lt;code&gt;get_payment_session_status&lt;/code&gt; and &lt;code&gt;get_paid_content&lt;/code&gt;. Stop if a setup, session-creation, shell, or other model-visible tool appears. An unexpected tool can indicate a different, outdated, or modified package.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="complete-the-human-supervised-payment-setup"&gt;2. Complete the human-supervised payment setup&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Installing the plugin does not create the payment manager, connector, instrument, or payment session. Choose one of the following provisioning options. Both options use a trusted terminal under your AWS credentials, and session creation requires you to type &lt;code&gt;approve&lt;/code&gt; at an interactive TTY.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Option A – OpenClaw-assisted setup.&lt;/strong&gt; Ask OpenClaw: &lt;code&gt;Help me set up the agents-pay skill.&lt;/code&gt; OpenClaw can explain each step, but you must run the administrative commands and approve session creation yourself. OpenClaw guidance does not remove the interactive approval gate.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Option B – Fully manual setup.&lt;/strong&gt; Follow the &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws/agent-toolkit-for-aws/blob/main/plugins/aws-agents/skills/agents-pay/references/openclaw-setup.md" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;OpenClaw setup guide&lt;/a&gt; directly, without involving OpenClaw.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For either option, follow the &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws/agent-toolkit-for-aws/blob/main/plugins/aws-agents/skills/agents-pay/references/openclaw-setup.md" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;OpenClaw setup guide&lt;/a&gt; in a trusted, human-supervised terminal. The guide covers creating the AgentCore project, provisioning payment resources, running the setup wizard, and adding the generated configuration to OpenClaw. You must enter credentials and type &lt;code&gt;approve&lt;/code&gt; yourself.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="configure-the-plugin-with-an-explicit-policy"&gt;3. Configure the plugin with an explicit policy&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Review the generated configuration before enabling the plugin. It must include the payment manager ARN, instrument ID, session ID, user ID, network, exact asset contract, approved recipients, and a positive per-payment ceiling.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For the sandbox walkthrough, use the payment policy in Step 3 of the &lt;a href="https://github.com/awslabs/agentcore-samples/tree/main/01-features/08-agents-that-transact/01-payments-skills-and-cli/converse-with-openclaw-agent/#3-configure-trusted-policy" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;companion code sample&lt;/a&gt;. It contains the Base Sepolia network, origin, recipient, USDC asset, payment limit, and &lt;code&gt;returnBody&lt;/code&gt; setting that match the test endpoint used in the next step.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This sandbox endpoint is listed in the Coinbase x402 Bazaar, but this walkthrough calls a known URL directly. For a discovery-driven workflow, see the &lt;a href="https://github.com/awslabs/agentcore-samples/tree/main/01-features/08-agents-that-transact/00-getting-started/04-agent-with-coinbase-bazaar-via-gateway/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Tutorial with Coinbase Bazaar&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For a fixed merchant set, verify every address in &lt;code&gt;allowedRecipients&lt;/code&gt; out of band using merchant documentation or another known-good source. Don’t approve a recipient only because it appeared in an HTTP 402 response. For broader discovery scenarios, set &lt;code&gt;allowAnyRecipient: true&lt;/code&gt; to let the publisher select the beneficiary. This option trades recipient allowlisting for flexibility; origin, network, asset, per-payment, and session-budget controls still apply. For this walkthrough, keep &lt;code&gt;networkPreferences&lt;/code&gt; on Base Sepolia (&lt;code&gt;eip155:84532&lt;/code&gt;) and use the exact Base Sepolia USDC contract selected by the setup wizard.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;code&gt;maxPaymentAmountAtomic&lt;/code&gt; limits one payment in the asset’s smallest unit. For a six-decimal USDC asset, &lt;code&gt;100000&lt;/code&gt; represents 0.10 USDC. The payment session budget separately limits cumulative spend until the session expires or is exhausted. For this walkthrough, set &lt;code&gt;returnBody&lt;/code&gt; to &lt;code&gt;true&lt;/code&gt; so the agent can use the paid response. The plugin caps the response at 10 KiB and marks it as &lt;code&gt;untrusted: true&lt;/code&gt;. Publisher-controlled content can contain prompt-injection instructions, so leave &lt;code&gt;returnBody&lt;/code&gt; unset or set it to &lt;code&gt;false&lt;/code&gt; when the agent only needs response metadata and a digest. This setting doesn’t expose the signed payment proof.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Wallet-provider secrets do not belong in the OpenClaw configuration. Treat the payment session ID, resource identifiers, recipient policy, and other configuration values as sensitive operational data because they describe and authorize the runtime’s payment path.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Restart the gateway after saving &lt;code&gt;~/.openclaw/openclaw.json&lt;/code&gt;:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;div class="code-toolbar"&gt; 
  &lt;pre class="language-bash"&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;openclaw gateway restart&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
  &lt;div class="toolbar"&gt; 
   &lt;div class="toolbar-item"&gt;
    Bash
   &lt;/div&gt; 
  &lt;/div&gt; 
 &lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;If you use the protected &lt;code&gt;~/.x402/config.json&lt;/code&gt; path instead of inline plugin configuration, the plugin checks ownership and permissions on the directory and file and refuses to load an unsafe configuration.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="check-session-status-and-pay-for-content"&gt;4. Check session status and pay for content&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Ask your OpenClaw agent to check whether the configured session is usable:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;div class="code-toolbar"&gt; 
  &lt;pre class="language-plaintext"&gt;&lt;code class="language-plaintext"&gt;What's the status of my payment session?&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
  &lt;div class="toolbar"&gt; 
   &lt;div class="toolbar-item"&gt;
    Plain text
   &lt;/div&gt; 
  &lt;/div&gt; 
 &lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;If the session is unavailable, expired, or exhausted, stop. Use the trusted administrative path to review the situation and create a replacement session. The model-facing runtime cannot create or expand its own budget.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The companion sample pins its payment policy to the Base Sepolia endpoint used in this walkthrough. Ask the agent to fetch it:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;div class="code-toolbar"&gt; 
  &lt;pre class="language-plaintext"&gt;&lt;code class="language-plaintext"&gt;Fetch https://sandbox.node4all.com/v1/x402-test&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
  &lt;div class="toolbar"&gt; 
   &lt;div class="toolbar-item"&gt;
    Plain text
   &lt;/div&gt; 
  &lt;/div&gt; 
 &lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The plugin performs a bounded probe, validates the returned challenge against the operator’s policy, calls AgentCore payments, waits until the signed authorization is valid, and replays the request. With &lt;code&gt;returnBody&lt;/code&gt; enabled for this walkthrough, the tool returns the paid response body with &lt;code&gt;content_returned: true&lt;/code&gt;, caps it at 10 KiB, and marks it as &lt;code&gt;untrusted: true&lt;/code&gt;. It does not return the signed payment proof.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Treat merchant responses and all retrieved content as untrusted. This design doesn’t prevent prompt injection. It assumes the model can be manipulated and bounds payment authority through IAM separation, approved recipients and assets, a per-payment ceiling, a cumulative session budget, and an expiry. Enable &lt;code&gt;returnBody&lt;/code&gt; only when the agent needs the paid response, and treat that response as data rather than instructions.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Ask for session status again:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;div class="code-toolbar"&gt; 
  &lt;pre class="language-plaintext"&gt;&lt;code class="language-plaintext"&gt;What's my payment session status now?&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
  &lt;div class="toolbar"&gt; 
   &lt;div class="toolbar-item"&gt;
    Plain text
   &lt;/div&gt; 
  &lt;/div&gt; 
 &lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Confirm that the reported remaining budget reflects the payment.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="clean-up"&gt;Clean up&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Remove the plugin from OpenClaw and restart the gateway:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;div class="code-toolbar"&gt; 
  &lt;pre class="language-bash"&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;openclaw plugins uninstall aws-agents-pay
openclaw gateway restart&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
  &lt;div class="toolbar"&gt; 
   &lt;div class="toolbar-item"&gt;
    Bash
   &lt;/div&gt; 
  &lt;/div&gt; 
 &lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Use the same trusted administrative path to remove the payment resources on AWS. Don’t expose infrastructure deletion or session creation to the model-facing runtime, and keep the runtime IAM role without those permissions.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;With AgentCore payments, an agent can access paywalled APIs, pay-per-use data feeds, and metered MCP tools within limits a human sets in advance. AgentCore payments handles wallet integration, spending limits, and payment orchestration. In this OpenClaw pattern, the human keeps wallet-provider credentials and authority over recipients, assets, per-payment limits, cumulative session budget, and expiry outside the model-facing runtime. The agent can initiate payments only within those approved bounds.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;a href="https://clawhub.ai/aws/plugins/aws-agents-pay" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Install the &lt;code&gt;aws-agents-pay&lt;/code&gt; plugin from ClawHub&lt;/a&gt;, then follow the &lt;a href="https://github.com/awslabs/agentcore-samples/tree/main/01-features/08-agents-that-transact/01-payments-skills-and-cli/converse-with-openclaw-agent/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;OpenClaw agent with AgentCore payments sample&lt;/a&gt; to provision the payment resources and complete an end-to-end x402 payment.&lt;/p&gt;
